Arunachala Mahatamya, Purvardha, 7.9.

Publisher’s Note
“Arunachala welcomes those rich in austerity based on wisdom 
(Jnana Tapas)”; this is the glory that Arunachala has gained through 
the gift of  His own grace. Sri Ramana Maharshi awoke to the reality 
of  Arunachala when he was sixteen years old. He came there drawn 
by the grace of  Arunachala, the mountain-magnet, after a death-
experience which resulted in Self-realisation. In the recent past Guhai 
Namasivaya, Guru Namasivaya, Isanya Desikar, Seshadri Swamy, 
Ramana Maharshi and many others have come to Arunachala from 
distant places to live and dwell as one with Arunachala, the Primal 
Being. This very thought uplifts our mind towards His eternal 
presence.

Specific references to Arunachala occur in various Sanskrit texts of  
antiquity. Ramana Maharshi gathered all the references to Arunachala 
from five sources in Sanskrit and copied out the 2659 verses with his 
own hand. This Sanskrit collection, called Arunachala Mahatmya, was 
translated into English and published by Sri Ramanasramam in 1957 
as The Glory of  Arunachala. 

Apart from the Sanskrit verses of  Arunachala Mahatmyam, Ramana 
Maharshi  was also familiar with a Tamil poetical work on the 
glory of  Arunachala called Arunachala Puranam.  While reading or 
narrating Arunachala Puranam, tears of  joy would flow from his eyes. 
The Arunachala Puranam is an exquisite original composition in Tamil 
verse, by Saiva Ellappa Navalar who lived about three hundred years 
ago during the reign of  the Tanjavur Nayaks. It enjoys the status of  a 
Sthala Purana in Tamil, dealing with the ‘history’ of  holy Arunachala 
(Tiruvannamalai). (Sthala Puranas record divine events, royal exploits 
and acts of  piety, all relating to a particular sthala, holy place of  
pilgrimage.)



x Arunachala Puranam

The vast majority of  the verses selected by Bhagavan are taken from 
the Sanskrit Arunachala Mahatmyam, which is also the major source 
of  the Tamil Arunachala Puranam. Therefore it is a matter of  great joy 
and celebration that many of  the verses he so long ago selected from 
the Sanskrit are now being echoed in this new work in an English 
translation from Bhagavan’s own beloved Tamil tongue. Many of  
these fascinating correspondances have been illustrated in the 
Introduction, and by means of  notes in the course of  the text itself.

Robert Butler, an English devotee and a Tamil scholar, has 
faithfully translated the letter and spirit of  the original Arunachala 
Puranam from Tamil to English for the benefit of  English readers. 
That the devotional fervour of  Saiva Ellapa Navalar has guided 
the pen of  Robert Butler becomes very clear from the following 
quote found in the book, ‘One thing that is striking in all these 
conversations is the fact that Bhagavan takes these Puranic 
records to be the literal truth, and not some literary invention’. 
He adds ‘The English translation of  the Mahatmya has a footnote 
stating that this is a ‘folk etymology only’, but Bhagavan’s quoting 
of  it appears to be evidence to the contrary’.

17th February 2015 Publisher
Maha Sivaratri Day Sri Ramanasramam



Foreword
This translation of  the Arunachala Puranam, the sthala purana of  
Tiruvannamalai, composed in Tamil by Ellappa Navalar, has been 
translated by myself, Robert Butler, and is offered for publication to 
Sri Ramanasramam as naivedya to Bhagavan Sri Ramana Maharshi. A 
short chapter has also been included which reflects on Bhagavan’s 
relationship with the mountain Arunachala, which, as the living 
embodiment of  Lord Siva himself, was throughout his life his one 
and only guru. 

Although the principal aim of  this book is to bring to the reader an 
accurate translation of  the Arunachala Puranam, I felt it would be a 
wasted opportunity to present it without reference to its major source, 
the Arunachala Mahatmya, a section of  the Sanskrit Skanda Purana. 
I would add that, in view of  the importance and esteem accorded 
it by Sri Bhagavan himself, some treatment of  its content is all the 
more desirable. Therefore a short introductory chapter has been 
added, which compares these two major works, pointing out some 
of  the differences and similarities in their respective treatments of  
the Arunachala story. Some quotes from the source work are also 
included as notes within the text itself. In many cases, when the 
Mahatmya is quoted, the original Sanskrit text, in transliterated form, 
is given alongside the English translation.For those who know Tamil, 
copies of  the Tamil text are easily available, and there is therefore no 
need for lengthy quotes in that language. However, it is quite likely 
that the vast majority of  those who know some Sanskrit will never 
have access to the actual Sanskrit text. Hopefully these quotations will 
enable such readers to get some feeling for the beauty and power of  
the language and style of  the original.






Introduction
The Arunachala Puranam

The Arunachala Puranam is the sthala purana of  Tiruvannamalai. A 
sthala purana is a work which brings together all the legends relating to 
a particular holy place, or sthala, as they are known. It was composed 
in the 17th century by Ellappa Navalar. Most sthala puranas, like the 
present one, are relatively recent in composition, but the myths and 
legends they contain form part of  an unbroken tradition, elaborated, 
embroidered and transformed over several millennia, beginning 
with the Vedas, and the Vedic commentaries, the Brahmanas, and 
culminating in the Puranic period with the two great epics, the 
Mahabharata and the Ramayana, the eighteen major Puranas, and the 
numerous minor Puranas, known as Upapuranas. Their subject matter 
is nothing less than the history of  the universe and everything in it 
as seen by Hindu society. To read the Arunachala Puranam, modest 
in scope though it is, is to take a cross-section through over two 
thousand years of  the evolving sensibility of  Hindu society. In the 
Arunachala Puranam, the main story unfolds against the background 
of  epic themes such as the creation of  the universe, and the battles 
for supremacy between the gods themselves, and the gods and their 
enemies, the asuras. 

 
Two major themes are interwoven: the first is the greatness of  the 
Arunachala sthala, and the story of  how the mountain Arunachala first 
manifested as an unfathomable pillar of  fire, to settle a quarrel between 
Brahma and Vishnu; and the second is the story of  how Siva’s consort 
Parvati came to merge with Lord Siva as half  of  himself, as the merged 
deity Ardhanariswara. As these two strands merge, the ultimate sanction 
of  the sthala’s greatness is given, as Lord Siva chooses it as the place 
where he will reveal his non-dual nature, and unites indissolubly with 
his own creative energy, or sakti, as personified by Parvati:

Manifesting as female and male, we have thus divided the 
beings of  this world. I became the lingam, and you the 
pedestal just below it. But what you think of  as ‘I’ and 



xiv Arunachala Puranam

‘You’ are not in reality two. Coexisting like the tree and 
its inner core, We dwell as one.
Arunachala Puranam, v. 397

One of  the major purposes of  the sthala purana, of  course, is to attract 
pilgrims by giving details, not only of  what spiritual benefit might be 
gained from visiting it, but also providing information on its geo-
graphical location and features, its temples, tanks and other places of  
interest, its holy festivals, and so on. Much information of  this kind 
can be gleaned from the Arunachala Puranam. Information is given in 
the notes identifying where possible the lesser known sites and loca-
tions. Until now, no full translation of  the Puranam has been available. 
It is hoped that this book, in addition to its literary and spiritual value, 
will have some practical use for those who visit Tiruvannamalai, giving 
devotees a comprehensive view of  the Arunachala sthala and its legends 
as a whole, and will help them gain a fuller and deeper appreciation 
of  the stories that are given piecemeal in other sources, such as the 
recorded conversations of  Ramana himself.

Saiva Ellappa Navalar

This work is generally attributed to Ellappa Navalar, or Saiva Ellappa 
Navalar, as he is also known, although some sources cite as the author 
Ellappa Nayinar who lived in the previous century. It was not until 
the 20th century that it was definitively established that the two were 
different people. He is thought to have lived in the reign of  the Tamil 
king Tirumalai Nayakar, whose dates are 1623–1659. His birthplace 
may possibly have been Tiruvannamalai, but whether it was or not, he 
is thought to have spent many years there in a mutt or math, which is 
the Hindu name for a monastic or similar establishment. Others give 
his birthplace as a place called Irātānallūr, near Tiruveṅkāṭu. Others 
think he may have been born in Jaffna in Sri Lanka, on account of  the 
large number of  words of  Jaffna origin that are found in his works. 
He is said to have belonged to the Velala caste, who were powerful 
agricultural landlords, the aristocracy of  the Tamil society of  that time. 
It is also said that at an early age he was sent to Dharmapura ātīnam, an 
important Saiva mutt in the Tanjore district, where he received initiation 
into the Saivite faith, and attained great literary and linguistic skills.



Introduction xv

In addition to the Arunachala Puranam, he composed a number of  other 
Puranas, and composed two long poems which are both extant, the 
Aruṇai Antāti and the Aruṇai Kalampakam. The latter of  these, a long 
poem in mixed metre and written in the style of  Tamil Akam poetry, 
is particularly highly regarded. Readers of  this translation may have 
gathered that Ellappa was very fond of  the pun as a figure of  speech. 
The following verse from the Kalampakam illustrates how he often uses 
this device to bring a touch of  lightness and humour to his serious 
theme. In Tamil love poetry a spurned lover may adopt the recourse 
of  threatening to dress up a palmyra branch as a horse, with flowers 
and bells and so on, and ride it through the village street, holding a 
picture of  his beloved and proclaiming her cruelty for all to hear. 
In this verse, Siva is imagined to be rejecting the idea of  riding the 
palymra horse on the grounds that those who desire a peṇṇai – woman 
as part of  themselves will have no desire for a peṇṇai – palmyra [branch]:

ஏறுடை அண்ணலார் ஏழை பாகனார்
ஆறணி சென்னியார் அருணை வெற்பரே
கூற�ொரு பெண்ணையே கூடவேண் டுவார் 
வேற�ொரு பெண்ணையே வெட்டு வார்கொல�ோ
The Lord who rides the bull, the Partner of  the Maid,
He of  Arunai’s Mount, who holds the Ganges in his locks
desires to unite with a pen(nai) as half  of  himself.
Would he cut [a branch] from some other kind of  pennai, then?

Tiruvaruṇai-k-kalampakam v. 76.

(The word peṇ, when placed in the accusative case, becomes peṇṇai, 
which is also the word for a palmyra palm).

Sources of  the Arunachala Puranam

The author of  the Tamil Arunachala Puranam, in verses 21 and 514, 
cites as the source for its first 7 chapters the Rudra Samhita (514), or the 
Kodi Rudra Samhita (21) of  the Siva Purana. For the remainder of  the 
work, he cites the Linga Purana. It may be noted that the Rudra Samhita 
is more usually described as an ‘unofficial’, non-standard section of  
the Skanda Purana, not the Siva Purana. It is not at all clear to which 



xvi Arunachala Puranam

text the author is referring. Some texts divide the Skanda Purana into 
sections called samhitas. Ellappa could therefore be using the name 
Rudra Samhita simply to refer to a larger section of  the Purana in which 
the Mahatmya is located. At the time of  writing no text or translation 
of  either the (Koti) Rudra Samhita or the Linga Purana was available to 
the translator, and therefore any discussion of  their role as a source 
for the present work will have to await a later edition. 

There is though one work which stands out as an obvious source 
for the majority of  the material directly related to the mountain 
Arunachala and this is the Arunachala Mahatmya – The Greatness of  
Arunachala. The Arunachala Mahatmya is a section of  the Maheswara 
Kanda of  the Sanskrit Skanda Purana. In actual fact Arunachala 
Mahatmyam consists of  two sections called the Purvardha and 
Uttarardha, each of  which relates the same material, but in a different 
order, with variations of  detail and emphasis. In some places the 
Tamil Arunachala Puranam provides a near word-for-word translation 
for material in one or other of  the Mahatmya sections; elsewhere 
there are quite wide divergences, as the author appears to embroider 
material that is only mentioned in a cursory manner in the Mahatmya, 
or skips over material that is treated more fully there. Whatever 
original text or texts the author, Saiva Ellappa Navalar, worked from, 
it is hard to imagine that their content differed widely from that of  
the work we now know as the Arunachala Mahatmya. The fact that the 
title of  the work refers specifically to the greatness of  Arunachala, 
and the fact that it consists of  a collation of  two accounts, different 
in style and clearly by different authors, might lead one to conjecture 
that the Mahatmya may well be a digest of  all the relevant material 
relating to Arunachala, gathered together from disparate sources, 
either within the Skanda Purana itself, or other Saiva Puranas, such 
as the Siva Purana and the Linga Purana. 

There is one major theme which does not form part of  the Mahatmya 
but is dealt with in detail by the Arunachala Puranam: this is the destruc-
tion of  Daksha’s sacrifice, the death of  Sati, and the subsequent events 
which lead up to Parvati’s marriage to Siva, including the burning up 
of  Kama. These events are not mentioned at all in the first part of  
the Mahatmya, and in the second part, are merely summarised in a 



Introduction xvii

few slokas, as a cursory recapitulation for the benefit of  the reader, 
(Uttarardha ch. 17, 1-11). This is of  course one of  the major stories 
connected to Siva, and is told in a number of  Puranas and other texts, 
principally the great Siva Purana itself. To his credit Ellappa relates the 
story with great gusto and poetical artifice as his third chapter, making 
it an excellent launching-pad for the next two large chapters, which 
lead up to the merging of  Parvati with Lord Siva.

In 1999 Sri Ramanasramam published a book called The Glory of  
Arunachala. The major part of  this work is an English translation by Sri 
M. C. Subramanian of  the Tamil translation of  the Sanskrit Arunachala 
Mahatmya, done by Sri Munagala S. Venkataramiah (later Sri Ramanan-
anda Saraswati), the compiler of  Talks with Sri Ramana Maharshi. Parts 
I and II are a translation of  the two sections of  the Mahatmya and take 
up 157 of  its 216 pages. Section III is a translation of  a portion of  Siva 
Rahasya, consisting of  a section of  the Upamanyu Bhakta Vilasam entitled 
Arunachala Mahima. It consists of  a brief  resume of  the Mahatmya, as 
related to the sage Tirujnanasambandhar, after the mountain had been 
brought to his attention when he was staying in a nearby town. Section 
IV consists of  two extracts from the Siva Purana; a section from the 
Vidyeswara Samhita, dealing principally with the manifestation of  Siva as 
a column of  flame, and a short extract from the Vidyasara Samhita, treat-
ing of  the importance of  annadana (the giving of  food) at Arunachala. 
None of  these contain any material which might suggest that they are 
an important independent source for the Arunachala Puranam. Section 
V consists of  the verses selected by Sri Ramana as evidence, in a lawsuit 
brought by the temple authorities against the government, intended 
to show that the mountain Arunachala was actually a Siva lingam, and 
therefore fell under the jurisdiction of  the Arunachaleswarar temple. 
Fifty-six of  the sixty lines selected by Bhagavan are from the Mahatmya, 
although he had equal access to material from the other sources. This 
in itself  is an indication of  its importance and authority as the major 
source of  the Arunachala Puranam.

The Arunachala Puranam and Arunachala Mahatmya compared

This short chapter comparing the Arunachala Puranam with the 
Arunachala Mahtmya has not been included for any academic reason, 



xviii Arunachala Puranam

such as for example, attempting to verify the sources of  the Tamil 
work, but simply out of  the desire to further embroider the rich 
tapestry of  the Arunachala story, as told in Ellappa’s work. 

Ramana Maharshi teaches us that the world is simply the illusory play 
of  mind, projected upon the unmoving screen of  consciousness. The 
bizarre and wonderful world of  the Hindu Puranas is in its own way 
no less an illustration of  this truth. Here the laws of  the so-called 
physical universe are suspended; the laws of  time, space and matter do 
not apply here. An infinity of  time can pass in a second, and a second 
can endure for an infinity. Myths within the Puranas are multi-faceted 
and contradictory across their multiple sources, the various strands 
intertwining and ramifying like thought itself. It is as if  the authors 
are warning the reader to beware of  the ephemeral realm of  the 
mind where nothing is as it seems, and nothing endures, apart from 
the underlying self, the Atman. Within this maze the one Ariadne’s 
thread is the pursuit of  dharma, which requires the abandonment of  
the ego and surrender to the law of  the universe, an endeavour that 
will surely, in the fullness of  time lead us back out of  the maze and to 
union with that supreme Self. Should anyone be in any doubt about 
the higher aims of  these Puranas, let them study the following slokas 
from the Mahatmya:

namaḥ sarvajagatkartre śivāya paramātmane, 
Obeisance to Siva, the Supreme Soul, the maker of the entire 
universe,	

māyayā śaktirūpeṇa pṛthagbhāvam upeyuṣe.
You who have assumed separate forms in the nature of saktis through 
maya!

	
eka eva mahādevo. na pare tvadvinā vibho.
Mahadeva is one and only one. O Lord, without you, none else exists!

	
Ar. Mā, Pūr. Ch.3, sl. 35 & 37b.

The Manifestation of  the Fiery Lingam

In the Mahatmya, the story of  the quarrel between Brahma and 
Vishnu and the subsequent manifestation of  Siva in the form of  a 
fiery lingam is very much like the account in the Puranam, except that 



Introduction xix

in the Purvardha there is no mention of  the screwpine flower and its 
bearing false witness on behalf  of  Brahma, and hence no mention 
of  any sanction against Brahma, forbidding his worship in temples. 
In that account, Brahma, as he becomes more and more tired and 
dispirited is addressed by some siddhas, described as being born of parts 
of his (Lord Siva’s) splendour – siddhās tejoṁśasambhavāḥ (Purvardha 1, 
55b), who point out the error of  his ways, wondering how Brahma, 
who has himself  issued forth from the Atman, along with crores of  
other suras, could possibly hope to perceive any limit to that from 
which he himself  was born. Brahma then comes to his senses and 
gives up his quest.

In both sections of  the Mahatmya, Siva’s appearance, as he bursts 
open the column of  fire and appears before Brahma and Vishnu, is 
described in greater detail than in the Puranam. The following is the 
account given in the Uttarardha:

tejaḥstambhaṁ vinirbhidya sandhyābhram iva candramāḥ, 
Bursting asunder the column of refulgence, like the moon wandering 
[out of the clouds] in the evening sky,
kailāsa-kūṭa-dhavalaṁ vṛṣendram adhitasthivān,
mounted upon a lordly bull, dazzling white, like the peak of Kailāsa,
jaṭājūṭavatā bāla-candra-cūḍena maulinā,
the hair upon his head matted and braided, with the crescent moon as the crest,
kapāla-mālikāṁ vaidhīm srajaṁ ca āragvadhīṁ dadhat,
wearing, as prescribed, a necklace of skulls of demons, and a garland 
of cassia fistula (Indian laburnum),
nāga-kuṇḍalibhiḥ phāla-phalaka-udbhāsilocanaiḥ pañcabhir vadanair 
dīptaiḥ, kṣveḍa-kalmāṣa-kandharaiḥ,
with five bright faces, each with serpents for earrings, an eye shining in 
its broad forehead and a throat marked by the stain of poison,
śūlaṁ, kapālaṁ, ḍamaruṁ, sāraṅgaṁ, paraśum, dhanuḥ, khaṭvāṅgam 
amalaṁ, khaḍgaṁ dorbhir nāgaṁ ca dhārayan,
holding in his hands a trident, a skull, a ḍamaru drum, a deer, an axe, 
a bow, a flawless khaṭvāṅga (skull-topped club), a sword and a serpent,
śvasitoddhūlitākāro gajacarma-uttarīyavān, sarvālaṇkārasampannaḥ, 
sarvadevair abhiṣṭutaḥ,
his body sprinkled all over with ash, wearing an elephant hide as an 
upper garment, adorned with all ornaments, praised by all the gods,
paridhānīkṛtavyāghracarmā tābhyām adarśi saḥ.
and wearing a tiger’s skin as his wearing cloth, he was seen by them thus.

Ar. Mā, Utt. Ch. 15, sl. 1-6a.



xx Arunachala Puranam

The Masking of  the Divine Eyes
In the account of  the sports of  Siva and Parvati after their marriage 
in Uttarardha there is no mention of  the masking of  Lord Siva’s eyes. 
The marriage goes well at first, until Parvati sees the Lord performing 
the sandhya rite with joined palms and closed eyes. The thought occurs 
to her that he is thinking about some other woman, not her, and her 
jealousy begins to grow out of  control:

dhyāyate nūnam adhunā kā api saubhāgya-śālinī. kriyate yad mayi prema 
tad manye vañcanaṁ mahat.

Certainly some fortunate woman is now being meditated upon [by him]. 
His love for me, I think, is a great fraud.

Ar. Mā, Utt. Ch.18, sl.3.

antar-vepa-dhutau tasyās ca kampāte payodharau, padma-kośau iva 
antaḥ-sthaḥ-cañcarīka-pracālitau.

Heaving with the internal tremor, her breasts quivered, like a pair of 
lotus buds, oscillated by the bees caught within them.

Ar. Mā, Utt. Ch.18, sl.13.

She decides to go and perform penance in order to win her husband, 
leaving whilst Lord Siva is still in meditation, lest he talk her out 
of  Her decision. Accompanied by some of  her handmaidens, who 
follow her despite being forbidden, she wanders with no particular 
destination in mind, until she sees, not far away, a red mountain with 
eight peaks, Arunachala.

In the Purvardha, the story of  the masking of  the eyes occurs in the 
same way as in the Puranam, but in the latter, there is no mention 
of  Siva’s reaction to the incident, and of  the ensuing conversation 
with Parvati regarding the tapas she is to perform, an incident which 
occupies 22 slokas in the Mahatmya. In this account Siva is gracious 
and indulgent towards Parvati, ascribing her conduct to youthful 
simplicity, and addressing her as mugdhe – O sweet innocent one! Siva 
is pleased with Parvati’s genuinely repentant attitude, but, since the 
destruction of  the worlds has been improperly brought about, at the 
wrong time and through the wrong agency, tapas must be performed 



Introduction xxi

to restore the universal order. As Siva and Parvati are in essence of  
the same non-dual nature, determining the correct course of  action 
requires some consideration:

manmūrtes tava keyaṁ vā prāyaścittiriha ucyate?
What expiation might be suggested here for you, who are of the same 
form as myself?				  

Ar. Mā, Pūr. Ch.3, sl. 50.
His final decision is that Parvati shall go and perform tapas on the 
earth, while he himself  replicates that tapas in his own formless form:

ahameva tapaḥ sarvaṃ kariṣyāmi ātmani sthitaḥ. pṛthvī ca sakalā bhūyāt 
tapasā saphalā tava.
I myself shall perform the entire penance, stationed in the Atman. The 
entire earth shall be fruitful through your penance.

ahaṃ ca niṣkalo bhūtvā tava mānasapaṅkaje sannidhāsyāmi. mā bhūs 
tvaṁ devi madvirahākulā.
I, becoming undivided and free of attributes, will be present in the lotus 
of your mind. My lady, do not become distressed due to separation from 
myself.

Ar. Mā, Pūr. Ch.3, sl. 55 & 65.

The story of  how Mahisa came to Parvati’s hermitage
The Arunachala Puranam tells us how daityas in the service of  Mahisa 
come upon Parvati’s hermitage by chance whilst hunting. When they 
surround it, presumably with the intention of  forcing entry, they are 
slain by the Bhairavas posted as guards by Parvati. When Mahisa hears 
of  this, he mounts his attack and a great battle ensues. However, both 
sections of  the Mahatmya give expanded, and quite different, versions 
of  this incident.

In Purvardha, chapter 10, some daityas come across Parvati’s hermitage 
whilst hunting with Mahisa, as in the Puranam. They chase some deer 
into the hermitage, but are prevented from killing them by the guards 
posted by Parvati, the Batukas (these are aspects of  Lord Siva in the 
sakta or tantric tradition). The Batukas, however, inform the daityas that 
a beautiful goddess is performing tapas there. By means of  their maya, 



xxii Arunachala Puranam

the daityas assume the forms of  birds and enter the hermitage, where 
they see Parvati, who, after being visited by the gods, who complained 
of  Mahisa’s conduct, has by now assumed the form of  a fascinating 
sakti, as part of  a plan to entrap and kill the asura:

gateṣu teṣu deveṣu gaurī kamalalocanā. babhūva mohinī śaktiḥ 
kāntiyuktā tatodarī.

After those gods had departed, lotus-eyed Gauri became a fascinating 
sakti, endowed with splendour and a concealed belly.

Ar. Mā, Pūr. Ch. 10, sl. 22.

The daityas report these events to Mahisa, who, struck by Smara (the 
god of  love), assumes the form of  an old man and enters the hermit-
age, where he is greeted with honour as a guest. When informed by 
Parvati’s attendants that she is performing penance to win back her 
husband, who is very powerful and hard to please, Mahisa laughs, 
saying that he is the husband she will gain as the fruit of  her penance; 
revealing his true identity and boasting of  his wealth and power, he 
attempts to take hold of  her. Parvati responds by assuming the fiery 
form of  Durga, riding upon a lion, and, with covert assistance from 
Brahma, Vishnu and Sadasiva, drives him away. 

Parvati’s first thought is to attempt to placate the asura, thus avoiding 
any violence if  at all possible. She calls the preceptor of  the suras, and 
asks him to assume the head of  a monkey, go to Mahisa and deliver 
a final ultimatum to him, advising him to forswear his evil ways or 
suffer the consequences:

mā eva tvam aruṇādrīśam upapīḍaya durmate. atra durmanasām vīryam 
adṛśyaṁ bhavati kṣaṅāt.

O vicious one, do not torment the Lord of Aruṇādri thus. Here the 
prowess of the evil-minded disappears in a moment.

pūrvajanmakṛtaiḥ puṇyair labdhavīryamahodayaḥ.
mā tvam śōṇācaleśāgnau śalabhatvaṁ bhaja asura.

By means of the meritorious deeds of previous births you have acquired 
great prosperity and prowess. O Asura, do not become a moth in the fire 
of the Lord of Sonachala!



Introduction xxiii

atra dharmātmanāṁ vāsaḥ śivabhaktimatāṁ sadā.
parapīḍāprasaktānāṁ bhavet rogaśatāvṛtah.

It is here that pious souls and persons endowed with devotion to Siva always 
live. This place is likely to be one infested with hundreds of ailments for those 
engaged in torturing others.
Ar. Mā, Pūr. Ch. 10, sl. 75, 77 & 79.1

These arguments, however, only make Mahisa more furious. The 
monkey-faced sage barely manages to escape, using his mayic power, 
and Mahisa gathers his armies for the forthcoming battle.

The Uttarardha gives a different and much briefer version of  the story. 
In this, Mahisa hears of  Parvati’s penance, and sends a messenger in 
the form of  a female ascetic to plead on his behalf. She uses her wiles 
to attempt to dissuade Parvati from her tapas, and convince her that 
only Mahisa would make a suitable husband for her:

tapojaḍo mṛḍo diṣṭyā prāgeva asti tvayā ujjhitaḥ. tava anurūpo na eva 
anyo vidyate diviṣatsu ca.

Fortunately Mrida (Siva, as the compassionate one), who has become 
sluggish from tapas, has already been abandoned by you, and it doesn’t 
seem that there is amongst the gods anyone else who is suitable for you.

Ar. Mā, Utt. Ch. 19, sl. 10.

Seeing that Parvati is infuriated by such distasteful talk, Vijaya (Durga) 
drives her out. The female ascetic, resuming her daitya form vows 
revenge and reports the matter to Mahisa, who gathers his armies to 
take Parvati by force.
1.	 These slokas have been paraphrased by Bhagavan as follows:

என்றுமே அறவ�ோர் அன்பர்க்கு இருப்பிடம் இத்தலம் தான்.

This sthala is always the abode of pious devotees. [Here] the wretched ones, 
who contemplate doing evil to others, will perish, after suffering many ailments. 
The power of the wicked [to do evil] will fail completely and disappear in an 
instant. Do not fall into the flames of the anger of the Lord of Arunai, who 
has assumed the form of a mountain of fire.

Aruṇācala Māhātmiyam, v. 7, The Collected Works of Sri Ramana Maharshi.



xxiv Arunachala Puranam

Parvati becomes the left half  of  the Lord

In verse 278 of  the Puranam Siva appears to Parvati after her penance 
in Kancipuram and asks her what boon she desires, to which she 
replies piṟitu oṉṟum illai; iṭa-p-puṟam taruti! – There is nothing other 
than this: grant me the left half [of yourself ]! In the Puranam there is no 
repetition of  this request by Parvati prior to the final merger with 
Lord Siva in chapter 5. In the Mahatmya however Parvati does make 
this request in an impassioned plea to Lord Siva, just prior to their 
final union in Tiruvannamalai. The following three slokas are taken 
from that plea for the granting of  the boon of  non-separation from 
the Lord:

atha gaurī purārātiṃ praṇamya jagadaṁbikā, 
ayācat tādṛśā śambhum avinābhāvam ātmanaḥ.

Then Gauri, the Mother of the universe, being of that sort (as described 
previously), worshipping the Destroyer of the cities, begged Lord Śambhu 
for non-separation from Himself.

na tyājyam etad te rūpam atra dṛṣṭimanoharam.  
ahaṁ tvayā na ca tyājyā sāparādhā api sarvadā. 
manoharam idaṁ rūpam etad te lokamaṅgalam.

This form of yours which is very charming should not be abandoned. 
I should never be abandoned, even if I am guilty of offence. This 
fascinating form of yours is conducive to the auspiciousness of all the 
worlds.

tvad prasādāt ayaṁ deva sugandhiḥ puṣṭivarddhanaḥ 
āvayoḥ saṅgamo, dṛṣṭo bhūyāt sarvārthadāyakaḥ.

Lord, through your favour let our union be highly fragrant, and the 
promoter of welfare, and be seen as the bestower of all wealth and 
prosperity.

Ar. Mā, Pūr. Ch.13, sl. 1, 3 & 8.
Ramana Maharshi and the Arunachala texts

There it stands, a mountain, insentient it would seem,
its wonder, its mighty works, to all mankind unseen.
From innocence of  youth, as mighty Arunachala, it shone



Introduction xxv

within my thoughts, but of  its meaning, knowledge had I none,
even when I learned that it and Tiruvannamalai both, were one.
It charmed my mind and pulled me on, into its very presence,
till, drawing near, at last I saw its vast unmoving essence.

Thus, in verse 1 of  Eight Verses to Arunachala, does Sri Bhagavan de-
scribe how, from his early youth, he was captivated by the mountain 
Arunachala, even before he knew exactly where and what it was. 
From the day that he came there at the age of  16, he adopted it 
as his guru, recognising it as the living presence of  Lord Siva. It is 
therefore no surprise for us to learn that he had a high regard for the 
literary works which tell of  the glory of  the mountain Arunachala, 
and of  the legends relating to it. Written records of  conversations 
with Sri Ramana demonstrate both his great love for the Mahatmya 
and the Puranam, and the pleasure he took in retelling the stories in 
his own words and speculating on various aspects of  them. What 
follows are a few excerpts from these conversations, with references 
where applicable to the places in the Puranam and Mahatmya where 
their themes occur.

In the following excerpt from Devaraja Mudaliar’s Day by Day with 
Bhagavan2 Bhagavan refers to chapter IV of  the Puranam, The Masking 
of  the Divine Eyes, vv 320-322, where Parvati, after performing tapas in 
Kasi and Kancipuram, arrives in Tiruvannamalai and makes her way 
to the ashram of  Gautama:

As I was entering the hall about 2-50 p.m., Bhagavan was reading out 
from the Tamil Arunachala Puranam the verses in which it is said that 
Gauri, after crossing the several streets of  Arunachalam, reached 
Gautama’s Asramam. When Bhagavan came to the verses dealing with 
Gautama’s joy at Gauri’s coming to his Asramam, Bhagavan could 
not go on, for tears filled his eyes and emotion choked his voice. So 
he laid aside the book and Mr Desai continued with his reading of  the 
manuscript. I may here record that I have noticed on more than one
occasion in the past how Bhagavan could not proceed with the 
reading of  any deeply devotional portions of  Tamil works such as 
Thevaram and devotional hymns of  Thayumanavar. This afternoon 
when I took from Bhagavan the above Arunachala Puranam and

2.	 Day by Day with Bhagavan, 12.12.45., Afternoon



xxvi Arunachala Puranam

referred to the portion which moved him so deeply and told him, in 
effect, that I had discovered his plight which he tried to hide from 
us all, he remarked, “I don’t know how those people who perform 
kalakshepam and explain such passages to audiences manage to 
do it without breaking down. I suppose they must first make their 
hearts hard like stone before starting their work.”

The next paragraph, as Mr Desai continues with his reading, refers 
to Chapter XI, The Expiation of  Sins, vv 612-619, in which the Eight 
Vasus perform penance to atone for their sin of  pride in boasting of  
their tapas in the assembly of  Brahma. Bhagavan appears to have had 
access to some other account of  this incident in addition to the one in 
the Puranam, since there is no mention there of  the Vasus remaining 
there in the form of  hills. The idea that each Vasu became one of  the 
radiating spurs that surround the hill is an interesting one.

In the course of  Mr. Desai’s reading of  the manuscripts, he read 
about the holy hill having eight faces. To a question of  mine whether 
as a matter of  fact this hill has eight faces, Bhagavan replied, “The 
purana says that the Ashta Vasus having flattered themselves in 
Brahma’s presence lost all their worth, and to regain it they came 
and did penance here all round Arunachala. They were given darshan 
by Siva at one and the same time by Siva assuming eight faces in 
this hill. All those eight Vasus are still in the shape of  eight spurs 
round this hill. What is meant by saying all those Vasus are still 
here as hills and doing penance round this holy hill, it is difficult 
to understand. Does it only mean they are living on these hills and 
doing penance, or are they themselves these hills?” 

These reflections lead Bhagavan to speculate on the locations of  
the Guardians of  the Eight Directions, and on the locations for 
Gautama’s ashram and Parvati’s tapas. One thing that is striking in all 
these conversations is the fact that Bhagavan takes these Puranic 
records to be the literal truth, and not some literary invention.

He added, “It is difficult now for us to locate where the Ashta 
Dikpalakas actually stood sentry, whether at the spots where the 
Ashta Dik Lingams are now found or whether the lingams are those 
which were installed and worshipped by them. We cannot be 



Introduction xxvii

sure where exactly Gauri did penance and where Gautama had 
his Asramam. But it would be safe to assume that Gauri did her 
penance in the region covered by Pavalakunru, Durga temple and 
Pachiamman Koil, and that Gautama’s Asramam must have also 
been near this region.”

Chapter IX of  the Puranam, and chapter 9 of  the Purvardha of  the 
Mahatmya speak at length of  the power and efficacy of  circumambu-
lation of  the holy mountain Arunachala. The following quote from 
Bhagavan, taken from Letters from Sri Ramanasramam by Suri Nagamma3, 
evinces an intimate acquaintance with the content of  those two chap-
ters. His description of  the etymology of  the word pradakshina, which 
does not occur in the Puranam, is an exact translation of  slokas 68b & 
69a of  chapter 9 of  Purvardha of  the Mahatmya:

pracchinatti prakāro ’gham, dakāro vāṁchitapradaḥ; 
kśikārāt kśīyate karma, ṇakāro muktidāyakaḥ.

The letter ‘pra’ cuts off sin; the letter ‘da’ bestows that which is desired;
the letter ‘kśi’ destroys karma; the letter ‘na’ bestows liberation.

The English translation of  the Mahatmya has a footnote stating that 
this is a ‘folk etymology only’, but Bhagavan’s quoting of  it appears 
to be evidence to the contrary. 

Bhagavan has not been keeping good health for some time past. 
Troubled in my mind on that account and unable to know what to 
do, I decided to go round the Hill, not only on Tuesdays as usual 
but also on Fridays and to pray to Arunachaleswara for Bhagavan’s 
health. With that decision, I went to Bhagavan on Thursday evening 
to tell him that I was going round the Hill the next morning, 
“Tomorrow? Is it Tuesday?” asked Bhagavan. 

“No. It is Friday,” I said. As if  he had understood my purpose, he 
said “Yes, yes.”

One of  the devotees who had recently come and had been staying 
for some time, asked Bhagavan, “Several people here go round 
the hill frequently. What is its greatness?” Bhagavan told him the 
following story: 

3.	 98. The Importance of Yatra and Pradakshina (Pilgrimage and Circumambulation).



xxviii Arunachala Puranam

“The greatness of  this Giri Pradakshina has been described at length 
in ‘Arunachala Puranam.’ Lord Nandikesa asked Sadasiva a similar 
question and Sadasiva narrated as follows, ‘To go round this hill 
is good. The word ‘Pradakshina’ has a typical meaning. The letter 
‘Pra’ stands for removal of  all kinds of  sins; ‘da’ stands for fulfilling 
the desires; ‘kshi ’ stands for freedom from future births; ‘na’ stands 
for giving deliverance through Jnana. If  by way of  Pradakshina you 
walk one step it gives happiness in this world, two steps, it gives 
happiness in heaven, three steps, it gives bliss of  Satyaloka which can 
be attained. One should go round either in Mouna (silence) or Dhyana 
(meditation) or Japa (repetition of  Lord’s name) or Sankeertana 
(Bhajan) and thereby think of  God all the time. One should walk 
slowly like a woman who is in the ninth month of  pregnancy. It 
seems Amba who was doing Tapas here, went round the hill on the 
day of  the Krithika star in the first quarter of  the night. Immediately 
after the Darshan of  the holy beacon, she became finally absorbed 
in Lord Siva. It is stated that on the third day after the festival of  
the Holy Beacon, Siva himself  started for the Pradakshina with all 
his followers. Really, it is difficult to describe the pleasure and the 
happiness one gets by this Pradakshina. The body gets tired, the 
sense organs lose their strength and all the activities of  the body 
become absorbed within. It is possible thus to forget oneself  and 
get into a state of  meditation. As one continues to walk, the body 
automatically gets harmonized as in the Asana state. The body 
therefore becomes improved in health. Besides this, there are several 
varieties of  medicinal herbs on the hill. The air that passes over 
those herbs is good for the lungs. As there is no vehicular traffic 
there is no worry about making way for cars and buses. One can 
walk care-free according to one’s wishes.”

On page 51 of  this book there is an inset giving a translation of  a sloka 
from the Mahatmya, followed by a translation into Tamil of  that sloka, 
composed by Sri Bhagavan. Several books record Ramana’s fascinating 
account of  the origin of  this translation. In A Sadhu’s Reminiscences A. 
M. Chadwick (Sadhu Arunachala) records the story as told to him 
by Devaraja Mudaliar, who had forgotten to include it in any of  his 
books; Kunju Swami recalls the same story in his Reminiscences (Enadu 
Ninaivugal ), and Suri Nagamma records it in Letters. In these cases the 
visiting monk is a sannyasin or sastri from Sringeri Mutt. Substantially 
the same story is also recorded by T. K. Sundaresa Iyer in At the Feet 



Introduction xxix

of  Bhagavan, although in this case the visiting sadhu is a Vaisnavite. 
The following is the account given by Suri Nagamma4:

A devotee who had heard about the meetings of  the Head of  the 
Sivaganga Mutt and the discussions about Narasimha Bharathi asked 
Bhagavan, “It seems a long time ago someone from the Sringeri Mutt 
requested Bhagavan to accept Diksha (formal initiation).” 

Bhagavan: “Yes. That is so. That was during the early days of  my 
stay at the Virupaksha cave. A Sastri residing in the Sringeri Mutt 
came to see me one morning. He saw me, spoke to me for a long 
time, and before going to the town for meals, drew near me and with 
folded arms and great respect said, ‘Swami! I have a request to make. 
Please hear me.’ When I asked him what it was, he said, ‘Swami, as 
you are born a Brahmin, should you not take Sanyasa in the regular 
way? It is an ancient practice. You know all that. What is there for 
me to tell you? I am anxious to include you in the line of  our Gurus. 
Hence, if  you give me the permission, I will come here with all the 
requisite articles from my Mutt and give you the initiation. If  you do 
not care to wear the full ochre-coloured robes (Akhanda Kashayam) 
I respectfully submit that it is enough if  your loin cloth at least is 
of  ochre colour. You may think over this well and give me a reply. 
I am going down the hill to take my meals and will come back by  
3 p.m. All the members of  our Mutt have heard of  your greatness and 
I have come here to see you at their request. Please do this favour.’ 

“A little while after he left, an old Brahmin came there with a bundle. 
His face appeared familiar. It could be seen from the outside of  the 
bundle that there were some books in it. As soon as he came, he 
placed the bundle opposite to me and like an old acquaintance said, 
‘Swami, I have just come. I have not had a bath. There is no one to 
look after this bundle. I am therefore leaving it with you.’ So saying 
he left the place. As soon as he went away, why, I do not know, but 
I felt like opening that bundle and seeing the books. As soon as I 
opened it, I saw a Sanskrit book in Nagari characters with the title 
‘Arunachala Mahatmyam.’ I did not know before that the Arunachala 
Mahatmyam is in Sanskrit also. I was therefore surprised and as I 
opened the book I found the Sloka describing the greatness of  this 
place in the words of  Iswara: 

4.	 66. Acceptance of Diksha.



xxx Arunachala Puranam

(See page 51 for the original Sanskrit text and Bhagavan’s Tamil 
translation).

(Those who live within 24 miles of  this place, i.e. this Arunachala Hill, 
will get My Sayujyam, i.e. absorption into Me, freed from all bonds, 
even if  they do not take any Diksha. This is my order.) 

“As soon as I saw that Sloka, I felt I could give a fitting reply to 
that Sastri by quoting that Sloka and so hastily copied it out, for 
the Brahmin might come back at any moment, and then tied up the 
bundle as before after replacing the book. I showed this Sloka to 
the Sastri as soon as he came in the evening. As he was a learned 
man, he did not say anything further but with great reverence and 
trepidity saluted me, went away and, it seems, reported everything 
to Narasimha Bharathi. Narasimha Bharathi felt very sorry for what 
his disciples had done, and told them to stop all further efforts in 
that direction. I subsequently translated that Sloka and wrote it in 
a verse in Tamil, ‘Yojanai Munra Mittala Vasarku’. It has now been 
added at the beginning of  the five verses in praise of  Arunachala 
(Arunachala Sthuthi Panchakam). In the same way, many people tried 
to convert me to their path. So long as it was mere talk, I used to say, 
‘Yes, yes,’ but never agreed to take any initiation. I always used to 
find some ruse to escape. Even the writings of  verses too is about 
the same. I never wrote any of  my own accord. Somebody used to 
ask me on some pretext or other. And I used to write on some urge 
from within. That is all.” 

The book Letters from and Recollections of  Sri Ramanasramam consists of  
31 letters which were held back from the original English translation 
of Letters, plus a translation of  Suri Nagamma’s recollections, which 
were published in Telugu as Sri Ramanasramam Smrutulu. The follow-
ing excerpt records Bhagavan’s account of  how Parvati came to be 
merged with Lord Siva as the left half  of  Himself. Interestingly the 
detailed account given below of  how Parvati averted starvation for the 
inhabitants of  Kasi and gained the title Annapurna is not given either 
in the Mahatmya or in the Puranam. In the Mahatmya Parvati does not 
go to Kasi at all in expiation of  her sin of  covering the Lord’s eyes, 
but goes straight to Kancipuram and then on to Tiruvannamalai. In 
the Puranam she is mentioned very briefly as visiting Kasi first and 
alleviating a famine there before seeking leave to depart from the king 



Introduction xxxi

of  that land, but there is no further detail. Possibly it is given in the 
Telugu translation of  the Puranam, which, it appears from some of  
Bhagavan’s comments in Letters, differed significantly from the Tamil 
original, or in some other Sanskrit Puranic text. The issue of  Parvati’s 
distress at existing in a separate form from Lord Siva is dealt with 
above in the section entitled, Parvati becomes the left half  of  the Lord.

30-7-1948

Jagadeeswara Sastry came from Madras this morning and, while 
discussing the greatness of  Arunachala as a pilgrim centre, enquired 
of  Bhagavan, why Parvathi got the second half  of  the body of  Siva. 
Bhagavan thereupon told us the following story: 

“Once upon a time when Siva was seated comfortably on Mount 
Kailas, Parvathi came from behind and closed his eyes for fun. As a 
result, the whole world was plunged into darkness as the sun and the 
moon lost their lustre. People were terrified and shaken. Devotees 
prayed to Siva to save them from the catastrophe. Parameswara im-
mediately opened his third eye and saved the people from their agony. 
Parvathi got afraid and removed her hands. Parameswara did not 
say anything. But Parvathi trembled with fear. The Lord said gently, 
‘Devi, this was no doubt only a pastime for you, but, by that, the 
whole world was deprived of  the light of  the sun and the moon. See 
how much people suffered. You may think it is just for a moment, 
but a minute for us is an aeon for the world. Why this childishness?’ 

“After listening to that mild reprimand of  Parameswara, Parvathi 
realised her mistake, felt ashamed and prayed for permission to do 
penance for the expiation of  her sin. Parameswara said, ‘You are 
the Mother of  the world. You have no sin. There is no need for 
penance.’ She was not satisfied. She said she would set an example 
to the whole world and so would do penance, and after obtaining 
his permission, she started southwards. At that time, in the kingdom 
of  Kasi, there was famine for want of  rain and the people were 
suffering greatly as they could not get food. Seeing this on her way 
and taking pity on the people, Devi created a big mansion by her 
mere wish, took the name of  Annapurna, and with the vessel which 
never got empty, fed thousands of  people. Before long, her fame 
spread throughout the country. Meanwhile the king himself  found 
that his granary had become empty and was wondering what to do. 



xxxii Arunachala Puranam

When he heard of  the poor feeding that was being done by the lady 
Annapurna, he was greatly surprised at the ability of  a mere woman 
and, to test her, asked for the loan of  a few measures of  rice. He 
got a reply to say that there was no question of  lending but that he 
could come there and eat. With a wish to test her ability, the king 
and his ministers went there in disguise and ate the food that was 
given. When the king found the inexhaustibility of  the food that was 
being served all round, he immediately realised that it could not be 
by any human agency but must be by divine power. And so, after 
the meal, he went and fell at the feet of  Annapurna and said, ‘Great 
Mother, please live with us and grant us deliverance.’ Pleased with 
the devotion, the Holy Mother assumed her original form and said, 
‘My son, I’m pleased with your devotion. As I have stayed here so 
long, your country will be relieved of  the evils of  drought. You will 
now have rains and there will be no famine. I cannot stay here any 
longer. I must go south for my penance. Rule the people well and 
be happy.’ The king said, ‘Even so, you should be available to us for 
our worship.’ So the Mother agreed and left. That is the reason why 
she manifested herself  as ANNAPURNA and the place where she 
was is now famous as the Temple of  Annapurna. 

“From there she went to Kancheepuram in the South, made a 
Sivalinga of  sand, worshipped it and, on realising that she was free 
from sin, and at the prayerful requests of  the people manifested 
herself  there as Kamakshi. After that, she got upon the Bull and 
saying she would go to Arunagiri, she came here. Devi went first 
to the Goutama Ashram at the foot of  the Pravala Hill. Satananda, 
the son of  Goutama, saw her and full of  devotional fervour invited 
her and worshipped her as prescribed and requested her to stay on 
while he went to the forest to bring his father Goutama, who had 
gone to fetch the kusa grass. By that time Goutama had already 
started for home and, when Satananda saw him, he ran to his 
father with great excitement and told him that the Divine Mother 
had come to their Asrama. The whole forest in the twinkling of  
an eye became green and full of  flowers and fruits. Goutama was 
surprised and asked his son if  it was really so. Satananda with a 
faltering voice said, ‘Mother Parvathi herself  has come.’ Equally 
thrilled and elated, Goutama hastened to the place, saw Parvathi and 
worshipped her. After that the Devi performed penance according 
to the instructions of  Goutama for a long time when Mahadeva 
presented Himself  and said that He would give her whatever boon 



Introduction xxxiii

she asked for. Devi said with great respect that she would become 
half  of  Siva Himself. ‘I cannot live’, she said, ‘any longer with a 
separate body. If  separate, I may make another mistake like this 
and then undergo all the hardships of  penance and suffer the pangs 
of  separation.’ Parameswara therefore acceded to her request and 
so became as ‘ARDHANAREESWARA.’ This is how Amba the 
Mother of  the universe became one half  of  Siva.” 

While narrating this story, Bhagavan became visibly affected, tears 
welled up in his eyes and his voice faltered. On completing the 
narration he assumed a dignified silence.

The text

The text used for this translation is that of  the edition published in 
2000 by Sri Ramanasramam. This in turn is a republication of  an 
edition first published in the 1930s, a copy of  which is still extant 
with corrections which appear to be in Bhagavan’s own hand. But 
more of  that later. This early edition was much more than a printed 
edition of  the Tamil text. It has nearly 850 pages, and, in addition to 
the text itself, accompanied by prose summaries of  each chapter and 
a prose translation of  each verse, contains just about all the known 
extant poetical works composed in praise of  the mountain Arunachala, 
including works by the Tevaram authors, Manikkavacagar, Arunagiri-
nathar, Guhainamasivayar, Gurunamasivayar, Sivaprakasha Swamigal 
and the author of  the Puranam, Ellappa Navalar himself. There is 
also a biography of  Gurunamasivayar, and a few short articles on the 
Saivite religion and other relevant subjects. The current Tamil edition 
comprises the text itself, the prose summaries and translations, the 
Annamalai Venba of  Gurunamasivayar, and some information on 
the Tiruvannamalai festivals. There has never been a critical edition 
of  this text, referring back to the original palm leaf  MSS, assuming 
that at least one of  these still exists, but the text appears to be quite 
stable, with only a few minor variants amongst the various editions.
The handwritten notes added by Bhagavan, referred to in the previ-
ous paragraph, give a correction to a note which appears in the early 
editions. The note is to verse 118 of  the modern edition. In that verse 
it is stated that Siva appeared as a column of  fire to settle the quarrel 
between Brahma an Vishnu. The erroneous note says:



xxxiv Arunachala Puranam

*மாசிமாதத்திலே அமரபக்கத்துச் சதுர்த்தசி திதியிலே
இரவு மகா நிசிக்காலத்திலே ச�ோதியாகத் த�ோன்றினார்.
In the month of Māci, in the second half of the lunar month, on the 
14th day at midnight (i.e. Sivaratri) he appeared as a bright efful-
gence.	

However Bhagavan knew this to be incorrect from his study of  the 
Sanskrit texts, which say that Siva’s first appearance as a column of  
fire was in the month of  Mārkaḻi (mid-December to mid-January) 
in the nakshatra of  Ardra (i.e. when the moon was in the Ardra 
nakshatra – region of  the sky, near Betelgeuse), and that the date he 
emerged from the column of  fire to be worshipped by Brahma 
and Vishnu was that mentioned in the note, i.e. on the day of  the 
new moon in the month of  Māci, (mid-February to mid-March).

       Bhagavan’s handwritten corrections to v. 118 
Bhagavan accordingly made the following corrections: below verse 
118 (40 in his edition) he wrote:

*மார்கழி � திருவாதிரையன்று
*In the month of Mārkaḻi on the day of [the] Ardra [nakśatra]



Introduction xxxv

thus establishing the correct date for Lord Siva’s initial manifestation, 
and he amended the footnote as follows:

*மாசிமாதத்திலே அமரபக்கத்துச் சதுர்த்தசி திதியிலே
இரவு மகா நிசிக்காலத்திலே அச்சோதியில் உருவாகத்
த�ோன்றினார், அரி அயன் முதல�ோர் பூசை க�ொள்ள.

In the month of Māci, in the second half of the lunar month, on the 14th 

day at midnight (i.e. Sivaratri) he appeared with form within that bright 
effulgence, so that Hari, Ayan and the rest might receive [the boon of] 
puja [to Him].	

thus confirming the date of  Siva’s emergence from the column of  fire. 
The modern edition of  the Puranam, published by Sri Ramanasramam, 
has replaced that footnote with one which quotes Bhagavan’s transla-
tion of  a Sanskrit verse which confirms these dates. In The Collected 
Works it is the second verse in the section of  Bhagavan’s translations 
from the Arunachala Mahatmya:

ஆதி அருணாசலப்பேர் அற்புதலிங் கத்துருக்கொள்
ஆதிநாள் மார்கழியில் ஆதிரை; – அச் ச�ோதியெழும்
ஈசனைமால் முன்னமரர் ஏத்திவழி பட்டநாள்
மாசிசிவ ராத்திரியாம் மற்று.
That day on which the ancient and wonderful linga of Arunachala took 
shape is the nakśatra of Ardra in the month of Mārkaḻi. And the day on 
which Vishnu and the other gods worshipped the Lord who appeared 
within that bright effulgence is the day of Maha Sivaratri.

The reference to the manifestation of  Arunachala in the month of  
Margazhi during the Ardra naksatra is from Siva Mahapuranam Vidyeswara 
Samhita chapter 9 sloka 15. Neither the Arunachala Puranam nor the 
Arunachala Mahatmya gives any date other than that of  Siva’s appearance 
to Parvati on the Krittika naksatra in the month of  Karttikai.

About this translation

As mentioned in the previous section, each verse in the Tamil original 
is followed by a section of  prose giving the meaning. The author of  
this urai, as it is called in Tamil, was the renowned scholar Mazhavai 



xxxvi Arunachala Puranam

Mahalinga Aiyar. For the current Tamil edition the original prose ver-
sion was amended somewhat to reflect current idiom by Shanmuga 
Arunachalam. Unfortunately the prose versions are not a pata-v-urai, 
in which each word or group of  words is translated, and sometimes 
commented upon, individually, but rather a cross between a some-
what loose translation and a paraphrase, with sometimes a consider-
able amount of  added material which is not in the text. Therefore 
when difficulties arise in the translation of  some words and phrases, 
the prose version, because it does not address individual words and 
grammatical constructions, is not always able to provide a full resolu-
tion. Fortunately, I have been able to call upon the assistance of  Dr 
T. V. Venkatasubramanian, the principal translator of  Muruganar’s 
Padamalai and Guru Vachaka Kovai, to check, and correct or improve 
on my own efforts.

Regarding the added material mentioned above, I have not included 
it in my translation except where it is necessary to do so in order to 
make a clear and grammatical version of  the original. The original 
Tamil is terse and economical in style and often requires a degree of  
‘fleshing out’ to make it read well in English. Therefore I have in places 
added words which are in the urai but not in text, and also words of  
my own which are not in either. As a general rule any words taken 
from the urai are placed in square brackets, and my own additions in 
round ones. The main purpose for the use of  these brackets is to avoid 
confusion for anyone who is following the translation in the original 
Tamil. Hopefully those who are only concerned with the translated 
text will be able comfortably to ignore them.

Proper names in the text, of  which there are very many indeed, are 
given in the form which most easily replicates their English pronun-
ciation without the use of  diacritics, e.g. Vishnu, Lakshmi. Where 
the names are Tamil versions of  Sanskrit words, the Sanskrit spelling 
has usually been preferred, as being more familiar to English readers. 
Few are likely to realise, for example that Urokini is the Tamil form 
of  Sanskrit Rohini. However in the notes, proper names, apart from a 
few familiar ones, like Śiva, Nandi, Viśṇu, have been given in italics in 
their Tamil, Sanskrit or both forms, using diacritic marks which permit 



Introduction xxxvii

a precise transcription of  each letter. See the Glossary and Index on 
p. 201 for information on how proper names have been presented 
there. Proper names in the text are given in normal case with a capital 
letter, whilst classes of  beings, such as suras, asuras, yakshas, vidyadharas, 
bhairavas etc. are given in italics, with no capital letter, just as ‘gods’ does 
not have a capital letter. A few though that are more individualised as 
gods, such as Rudras, Maruts, Vasus, Asvins, are treated as first stated. 
Some words which denote Hindu people, concepts and things, e.g. 
rudraksha, gopuram, tapasvin are also placed in italics. The meanings of  
these are given in the notes and / or glossary. A few notes have been 
added which will be of  interest only to readers of  the Tamil text. Such 
notes have been prefaced by the words Tamil Text : in italics. In works 
of  this kind there are invariably a number of  levels of  quotation, where 
someone is quoting someone else, and that someone goes on to quote 
yet someone else, and so on. Therefore a small note may be helpful on 
how quotation has been marked in this translation. As explained by 
the author in verse 21, the Arunachala Puranam is narrated by the rishi 
Suta, who is quoting the words of  Nandi. This base level of  narration 
has no quotation marks. When Nandi begins to speak, his words are 
enclosed in single quotation marks (‘...’), as are the questions posed to 
him by Markandeya. If  Nandi then goes on to quote someone else, 
this speech is enclosed in double quotation marks (“...”). If  this second 
person then quotes a third person, the quotes revert to singles ones 
(‘...’), and so on. For example, at some point in chapter 6:

Suta is quoting Nandi (‘...’)
who is quoting Kantisali (“...”)
who is quoting Durvasas (‘...’)
who is quoting Siva (“...”).

However, on the whole, there are rarely more than two levels of  
quotation, and where there are more than two, the length of  text at 
those levels is not extensive, and should not create any confusion for 
the reader. Any verse which is entirely part of  a quoted utterance, un-
less it begins or ends that utterance, is exempt from quotation marks. 
On the whole, the sequence of  events and identity of  the speaker is 
quite clear. Excessive use of  quotation marks would almost certainly 



xxxviii Arunachala Puranam

detract from the clarity of  the translation and invite confusion where 
none would otherwise exist.
 
Acknowledgements

I would like to express my thanks to Dr. T. V. Venkatasubramanian 
for reading the draft of  this book and providing many invaluable cor-
rections and suggestions. I would also like to thank David Godman 
for proof-reading the final draft, for providing sources of  informa-
tion for the section on Sri Ramana and the Arunachala Texts, and 
for permitting the reproduction of  material from his website relating 
to Vallala Maharaja. As usual, thanks are due to the President of  Sri 
Ramanasramam, Sri V. S. Ramanan for allowing me to quote exten-
sively from the Ramanasramam literature, and for accepting this text 
for publication by the Ashram. 



Arunachala Puranam

பாயிரம்

Dedication






Praise to Vinayakar

Three-eyed, vast as a mountain  
with an elephant’s head and trunk,
the threefold juices of  his rut, an ocean,
let us join hands in worship and praise of  Vinayakar,	
that his grace may bring harmony this day
to a history first told by Vyasa himself,
compiler of  the great Vedas.1	 (1)

Praise to Natesha

[Homage to] those mighty feet with ringing anklets [of  Him] 
who performs his dance, blighting the sun, 
cracking the universe’s outer shell2
and causing the bright earth to quake;
He who is adorned by the glorious padikams of  one skilled in Tamil, 
[Tirujnanasambandhar], whose songs
are imbued with a ripe sweetness;  
Flawless One;
Destroyer of  the births of  those who put their trust in Him.	 (2)

Greatness of  the sthala

That place where suns, moons, bhairavas, vidyadharas, gods, 
the Guardians of  the Eight Directions, 
the denizens of  the serpent realm, great tapasvins, rishis, 
lotus-dwelling Brahmas, and Vishnus with their noble garuda ensigns 
spread out over the earth in a dense crowd,
without even the space to bow down in worship;
where the cry harahara goes up, like the ocean’s roar,
is the rich and noble sthala of  Arunai!	 (3)

Here is a unique sthala where tender-breasted maidens dwell,
whose faces are framed by two ear-jewels,
like the bright moon, rising between two suns.
Richer than [Amaravati], the realm of  Indra, 



4 Arunachala Puranam

who wears a garland of  blooming [karpaka] flowers,
richer than the opulent realm of  Alaka, home to Kubera,
is the noble sthala of  Arunai.	 (4)

The divine home of  Him
who slew Yama with a kick as he rushed to the attack;
who reduced Kama to ashes;
whose throat is adorned with the poison halahala;
whose ornaments are the waxing crescent moon at eventide,
rearing serpents and the river Ganges;
whom Four-faced [Brahma] and the earth’s protector [Vishnu], 
could not find, though they sought Him;
who brandishes the trident in his hand,
is the sthala known as Sonagiri.	 (5)

Praise to Parasivam

Who is the unique First One, who has neither friends nor enemies?
Who is the Lord who creates the world once more 
at the time of  its dissolution?
Who is it that displays his divine dance upon the Vedas’ heads?
Who is the Knower behind all knowledge?
It is His two holy feet we worship!	 (6)

Praise to Arunachaleswarar

Unfailingly shall we hold in our thoughts
the One who became a unique mountain [of  fire];
He whose divine will it was to reveal his beauty,
transforming the fair universal shell
into a sphere of  pure gold,
as holy Mal, who slumbers in the deep, gem-strewn ocean,
and [Brahma], the Vedas’ Lord, suffered,
seeking Him upon the earth and in the heavens,
and in the infernal regions, where serpent nagas dwell.	 (7)



Dedication 5

He holds a deer in his hand 
and bears the maiden Ganga in his locks;
He keeps as half  of  Himself  the maiden Parvati, 
who in grace begets the seven worlds;
He fathered the elephant-headed [Ganesha],
who towers up like a tall mountain of  coral;
He joyfully bestrides a warring bull;
He is our noble Lord, Arunagiri,
whose holy feet we worship to free ourselves from birth.	 (8)

Praise to Unnamulai 

Her locks are like the clouds;
the look of  grace from her two eyes pours forth compassion;
her bright teeth shine like the crescent moon
in a fair countenance, scented with musk; 
her breasts are restrained by a bodice;
her [slim] waist is such as none have ever seen,
and her feet are dainty; 
She is Arunai’s Lady Unnamulai, 
on whom we fix our thoughts.	 (9)

Praise to Vinayakar who received a tribute of  elephants

He is the One upon whose elephant face 
the three juices of  the rut run like rivers;
He is the One who grants the fourth state,
beyond the other three3

to those who perform arduous tapas, 
planting his holy feet in their hearts;
He is the First One, fathered by [Lord Siva]
who destroyed the three cities4 of  the asuras;
He eradicates the births [created by] the three karmas 5.
Let us fix our thoughts upon [Vinayakar],
He who received in tribute a herd of  elephants.6	 (10)



6 Arunachala Puranam

Praise to Sivasubramanyar7

[He  dwells] at the tall [eastern] gopuram8 of  Arunai’[s temple],
girt by groves, thick with clustering blossoms;
riding upon the peacock, He dwells in the northern avenue;
He brings prosperity to all who seek his protection;
to Indra, whose rutting elephant stands tethered to its post,
He restored the shade of  the karpaka tree,
along with his golden [heavenly] kingdom;
He is Kumaravel  who wears a garland of  kadambu9 blossoms;
we shall place upon our heads 
the feet of  those who worship Him.	 (11)

Praise to the Three10

We worship the feet of  [Tirujnanasambandhar], the Lion 
[who defeated] the heretics with their [false] disputations;
we praise the holy feet of  [Appar], the rich Ocean of  Tamil, 
who was foully bound [by the Jains] to a great stone column,
but crossed the dark ocean [using it as a raft];
and we sing the glory of  Sundarar, the friend of  Lord Siva,
who sent our Lord as a messenger, hurting his holy feet,
to the home of  the maiden Paravai,
whose glancing looks dart from ear to ear.	 (12)

Praise to Tiru Vadavur Adigal11

He traversed the ocean of  karma, so hard to cross,
and entered the great harbour of  our Lord in Perunturai;
the religion of  the Buddhists he proved to be false;
attaining the immaculate condition of  jivan mukti,
he is the seed which develops into final liberation;
He is Manikkavacagar, the wise one of  Vadavur,
whose holy feet we praise.	 (13)



Dedication 7

Praise to the holy devotees12

We praise the lotus feet of  Iyarpakai Nayanar,
who happily gave up his own wife to the Lord;
we praise the feet of  Kannappa Nayanar,
who offered to the Lord who ruled him 
food tainted by his own saliva;
lovingly, we praise the feet of  Tirunilakanta Nayanar
whom those tainted by evil karma cannot know;
we bow to the twin feet of  Ilaiyankudimara Nayanar,
who rules me, his devotee.	 (14)

We worship the twin feet of  Aiyadigal Kadavarkon Nayanar;
we bow to the holy feet of  [Cirutondar Nayanar],
who, for our Lord, chopped up and cooked his own son,
and made of  him a repast flavoured with ghee;
we praise the true feet of  Tirunirrucozhan13;
with love do we adore the feet of Ceraman Perumal Nayanar,
who sang the tiru kayilaya ñana ula [prabandham].
These learned bards and all the rest,
whose service is never false, do we worship.	 (15)

Praise to Nandi, Durga, Gautama and Candecurar Nayanar

We make obeisance to the holy feet of  Nandi;
we praise the feet of  Durga;
we place upon our heads as a garland the feet of  rishi Gautama,
who is like a fifth Veda, distilled from the other four;
we make of  our thoughts an abode for Candecurar Nayanar,
who, in cutting off  his father’s two legs,
gained the state [of  liberation] from which he could never be cut off;
we place upon our heads
the feet of  those who meditate upon Arunagiri.	 (16)



8 Arunachala Puranam

Author’s Apology

That I should speak a few feeble words
in the presence of  those learned poets,
whose songs excel in the clear expression
of  the eight moods of  the mind14,
and a knowledge of  all the learned texts
that need to be studied,
is rather like a mere gnat, demonstrating the art of  flying 
before the swan mounted by Brahma,
who gave forth the glorious Vedas,
or Garuda, upon whom Brahma’s father [Vishnu] rides.	 (17)

Will anyone find fault with water when it is mixed with milk?
Will anyone take exception to a simple thread
when it is used to string a flower garland?
And will the great ones pour scorn 
upon my [feeble] words,
when they possess the distinction
of  expressing the name of  Arunai’s Lord,
He whose matted locks are adorned with aatti flowers,
and whose throat holds the poison [halahala]?	 (18)

Just because a poisonous tree is very old,
does that make its pretty fruits edible?
And just because delicious tasting sugarcane
is newly grown, does that detract from its savour?
[Just so,] in the case of  a badly written song,
or a verse composed with great distinction,
would age make the bad song good,
or newness make the good verse bad,
in the eyes of  those capable of  judging their worth?	 (19)
Those maidens whose divine forms 
are as tender as the aniccam flower,
and whose eyes are like death-dealing blue water lilies,
though they can withstand the flowery arrows
of  bow-wielding Kama,



Dedication 9

will not be able to tolerate
the arrows discharged in a (real) battle.
[Similarly] those whose learning and ability is such
that (their works) possess a great sweetness,
untainted by any hint of  a flaw in Tamil expression –
will they bear to stand [listening to] 
the ‘great’ wisdom of  fools, 
who are ignorant of  the subtleties of  grammar
that [others] have [been at great pains to] devise?	 (20)

The history of  this work

(In this work) I tell of  the greatness of  Arunagiri,
which is recounted in the Kodi Rudra Samhita15 of  the Siva Purana.
The Lord recounted it to Nandi,
whose mind melted in bliss;
Nandi recounted it to Markandeya;
Markandeya repeated it to rishi Vyasa,
[compiler of] the ancient Vedas;
Vyasa told it to Suta,
and Suta recounted it to the great rishis [of  the Naimisa forest].16	 (21)
 
I shall tell of  the greatness of  Arunai’s city;
how the mountain of  fire manifested to cure Brahma of  his egoism;
how [Dakshan’s] incomparable sacrifice was destroyed;
how Lord Siva married Parvati,
and how Parvati covered Lord Siva’s eyes;
how Parvati slew the demon Mahisa,
and how she merged with the Lord as his left side;
and how Vajrangada’s twofold karma was dispelled.
These stories and all the rest too shall I recount. 	 (22)

That which was written 
in the sweet northern tongue [of  Sanskrit]
I have told in the southern language [of  Tamil]:
of  the rare devotion and service
of  King Vallala, scion of  the race of  Agni;



10 Arunachala Puranam

of  pradakshina of  the holy mountain,
and of  its deeply purifying holy waters;
of  the boon won by the Sun;
of  renowned King Pradatta,
and of  how King Pulaka was released 
from the bonds of  karma. 	 (23)



References

1.	 Vyāsa, also known as Veda Vyāsa is also credited with authorship of the great 
epic Mahābhārata and the Purāṇas. He is also known as the one who divided 
the primordial Veda into its separate sections. The word vyāsa means divider 
or compiler.

2.	 These two lines probably refer to incidents in the Purāṇam, the first in chapter 
12, where the sun is punished for crossing the summit of Aruṇācala, and the 
second in chapter 2, where Lord Śiva manifests as a column of fire.

3.	 The fourth state referred to is that of identity with god, cāyucciyam, Sanskrit 
cāyujya. This is the highest of four patavi – states which express the nearness 
of the devotee to god.  The other three, in ascending order, are calōkam – being 
in god’s realm, camīpam – being near to god and carūpam– having the form of 
god.

4.	 The acutal text says, vidam muṉṟu puram – the cities of three kinds. The three 
cities of the asuras, destroyed by Lord Śiva, were made of iron, silver and gold 
and were located respectively on earth, in the sky and in heaven.

5.	 cañcitam muṉṟu piṟappu – the three births starting with sanchita. Karma which 
is the cause of births, is of three kinds: sanchita karma is the accumulated actions 
of the soul in former births, the fruits of which are experienced in future births; 
prarabdha karma is the part of one’s karma that is to be worked out in this life; 
agamiya karma is the actions in the present life which are the source of future 
births and the fruits to be experienced therein.

6.	 The reference is to Vināyakar’s encounter with a foreign king called Mukilaṉ 
(a general term for Moghul), who was harassing the devotees in the environs 
of Aruṇācala. Vināyakar appeared to him in a dream and transformed him into 
an elephant, or at least, frightened him badly. When Mukilaṉ  awoke he realised 
his error and rounded up a herd of elephants as a tribute to Vināyakar. This 
incident is recounted in one of the Tamil biographies of the famous Aruṇācala 
saint, Guhainamaśivāyar, who invoked Lord Śiva’s intervention against the 
king with a venba verse. In response Lord Śiva sent Vināyakar to scare the 
king. The story is recounted in the as yet unpublished translation of the saint’s 
extant works, edited by David Godman.

7.	 Civacuppiramaṇiyar, in Sanskrit Śivasubramaṇya is a name of Śiva’s younger 
son, Murukaṉ.  The word means noblest of Brahmins and is also used of Viṣṇu 
and others. This name appears only in the title to the verse.  In the verse itself 
Murukaṉ is referred to as Kumaravēḷ.  The words kumara and vēḷ are respectively 
Sanskrit and Tamil words for a young man.

8.	 The temple referred to here is probably the Kopurattiṉaiyaṉar koyil, which is at the 
foot of the second gōpuram – entrance tower in from the eastern entrance of the 



12 Arunachala Puranam

main Aruṇācaleśvarar temple.  Called the Vallāḷa gōpuram (see chapter 7, Vallāḷa 
Maharaja), it is the one from which the sage Aruṇagirināthar famously attempted 
to jump to his death, only to be saved by Lord Śiva. The temple mentioned later 
in the verse as being in the northern avenue is the Vaṭakku vīti Cuppiramaṇiyar 
koyil.

9.	 The kaṭampu is a flowering tree, which is of two kinds, the red species, 
ceṅkaṭampu and the white species, veṅkaṭampu, both sacred to Murukaṉ.

10.	 The reference is to Appar, Cuntarar and Tiruñāṉcampantar, the three most 
prominent of the 63 Tamil saints, the Nāyaṉār or Nāyaṉmār. Their combined 
works are called the Tēvāram, and are regarded as the Tamil Veda. Their 
biographies are told in the Periya Purāṇam. See v.15 note 13. See also the 
Bibliography for details of books which relate their stories in translation.

11.	 Māṇikkavācakar is the fourth of the great quadrumvirate of Tamil saints, along 
with Appar, Sundarar and Tiruñāṉcampantar, who revitalised and popularised 
the worship of Lord Śiva between the 6th and 9th centuries C.E., and were 
instrumental in the gradual decline and near disappearance of the Jain and 
Buddhist religions in South India. See the Bibliography for further details of 
his published biographies. Peruntuṟai means great harbour, and is the name 
of the seaport where, according to the saint’s biography, Lord Śiva appeared as 
a Saivite guru and personally gave him initiation.

12.	 This and the next two verses mention a number of the lesser known Nāyaṉār. 
The lives of all 63 saints are recounted in the Periya Purāṇam, composed by 
Cēkkiḻār.  See v. 15 note 13. 

13.	 The story goes that the Cōḻa king, Tirunīṟṟu Cōḻaṉ, otherwise known as 
Kulōttuṅka Cōḻaṉ II, was fascinated by the Jaina epic Cīvaka Cintāmaṇi. His 
chief minister was Cēkkiḻār who persuaded him that this work was no good for 
this life or the next, and aroused in him an interest in the Tamil saints. Inspired 
by what he read, the king asked him to compose an epic on the lives of the 
Tamil saints, the work we know as the Periya Purāṇam.

14.	 These are the eight rasa – emotions, expressed in dance, through gesture, and 
in poetry: vīram - courage,  accam – fear, iḻippu – disgust, viyappu – suprise, 
kāmam – love, avalam or karuṇai – sensibility, compassion,  kōpam – anger, 
nakai – laughter, sarcasm.  cāntam is sometimes given as a ninth rasa, meaning 
tranquility, equanimity, as exhibited by ascetics; others give vaṟcalam – parental 
tenderness as the ninth.

15.	 See the section entitled Sources of the Arunachala Puranam on p. xii.

16.	 The narrator is therefore the sage Suta, who is quoting Nandi most of the time. 
See the section entitled About this translation on p. xxxiv for an explanation 
of how quotation marks have been employed in the text.



Chapter 1

திருநகரச் சருக்கம்

The Holy City






He stands guard upon Mount Kailash’s silvery peak 
exercising the holy ordinance of  Lord Siva,
in whose locks the moon and the Ganges are intertwined;
his shoulders are four; 
he wears the Brahmin’s three-stranded thread;
his fair and holy loins are ever girt with a tiger’s skin;
his hair is gathered up in a knot;
his deep gaze is threefold; 
his body is smeared with white ash
so that it resembles a mountain of  red coral
over which the moon spills his pale light,
and his hands never loosen their grasp
on the deer, sceptre, sword and noble battle axe. 	 (24)

He [Nandi], whose cloud of  compassion 
pours down its rain to nurture the crop of  the Saivite faith, 
resembles the One [Siva] whose hue is that of  fresh lotus flowers,
whose holy throat is stained with black,
who rides upon a fine white bull.
To his father Siladan1 whose five senses are restrained within, 
he is like the life that animates him [Siladan] outwardly.
His lotus feet are made red by all the gods falling at his feet.	 (25)

Shining with the combined radiance of  the moon and river Ganges, the 
divine form of  the Lord himself, and the holy ash that so decorously 
adorns it, stands Mount Kailash, upon whose summit the gods with 
Indra at their head throng closely together, the tumultuous clash of  
crown upon jewelled crown dusting its slopes with a powder of  gold 
and precious gems, so that it resembles golden Mount Meru itself.    (26)

Atop this mountain stands a mantapam, with pillars of  diamond, sturdy as 
Mount Mandara, cornices of  rubies massed together, capitals carved from red 
coral, and cross beams of  emerald, its walls decorated with seamless mosaics 
of  sapphire gems, and its ceiling covered with slabs of  moonstone.	 (27)

It happened one time that Nandi was seated there upon a lion throne 
inlaid with pearls on either side, resting his lotus feet upon a noble 



16 Arunachala Puranam

footstool studded with bulky sapphires, holding court upon mighty 
Mount Kailash. He resembled Lord Siva himself, expounding the arcane 
secrets of  the Vedas to the assembled rishis, his right foot resting upon 
the back of  the demon Muyalagan2.	 (28)

Surrounding him on all sides were holy saints without number, amongst 
them, Kurcan3, Uromacan4 , Kumutan5, Kumutakan6, Cakatayan7, 
Kumbacatan8, Varcar9, Vaicambayanan10, Kanacimuni11, Pulikkalan12, 
Vamadevan13, wise Sanaka14, Sanatkumaran14, Vatarayanan15, Matangan16 
and he who performs puja to those dancing feet through all eternity, 
Anantamuni17.	 (29) 

Suddenly the deathless sage Markandeya sprang to his feet, made 
obeisance and spoke thus, ‘Peerless First One, who first made clear to
