After Mahisa had vented his anger in this manner, an asura by the name 
of  Nikumban spoke up, “Could such feeble beings withstand the anger 
you have voiced? With the weapon I hold in my hand I shall slay those 
that guard the grove where dwells the one whose breasts are like the 
tender, three-segmented coconut.” Speaking thus, he departed swiftly 
on his way.	 (342)

When that army of  footsoldiers, accompanied by tall chariots and dark 
elephants, rolled forward, it was as if  the Western Ocean, rising in anger, 
were setting out to do battle with the Eastern Ocean. As Nikumban 
came on, it seemed that, between northern mount [Meru] and mount 
[Pothiyam], home of  the great pitcher-born sage Agastya4, another great 
Mount Meru had grown up in their likeness.	 (343)

Mounted on a chariot drawn by a thousand steeds of  emerald hue, 
Nikumban came on, heading for Uma’s grove, the cloud of  dust that 
arose drinking up the ocean of  juices that poured from the temples of  his 
elephants. Espying them as they approached, like fire shooting out long 
tongues of  flame, and hurling many crores of  mountains, Nokkanangu 
and Thakkanangu5, bristling with weapons, confronted them in battle to 
halt their advance.	 (344) 

The eight Bhairavas6, Candan, Uruttiran, Coman, Kapali, illustrious 
Cankaran, Kotandan, Vithan, and the greatly esteemed Cittirangatan, 
let out a guffaw fit to crack the universal shell, and, like elephant-slaying 
tigers, destroyed in a trice that entire army, with the exception of  
Nikumban himself, his charioteer and chariot.	 (345)



Parvati becomes the left half  of  Lord Siva 107

Observing the destruction of  his army by the Eight Bhairavas and the 
two anangus, Nikumban rode to the attack, urging on his chariot so that 
it flew like a discus, and firing countless arrows in a tightly-knit lattice 
into the face of  Thakkanangu. But that Maid of  Heaven, brushing aside 
the hail of  arrows and felling his charioteer, plucked off  the arms that 
had discharged those arrows and sucked out Nikumban’s life.   	  (346)

A river of  blood flowed, as if  carrying with it the lives of  the asuras, 
separated from their bodies as they fell in battle. Fierce headless corpses 
danced, like demons, guaranteed a daily meal of  flesh. The Bhairavas and 
the two anangus put on victory garlands of  tumbai and vakai. Meanwhile 
messengers rushed to the buffalo-headed One to report the slaughter 
of  those asuras. 	 (347)

Upon hearing this, Mahisa turned to Kayidavan and said, “If  the word 
gets about that a little she-devil and her cohorts have slaughtered our 
great army, Brahma, Vishnu and He who bears an eye on his forehead 
will think twice before handing over any more tribute to us,” and, giving 
vent to a scornful laugh, he arose and mounted a chariot harnessed to 
a thousand elephants.	 (348)

Twelve illustrious asuras, each possessing the strength of  eleven crores 
of  elephants, mounted their chariots and urged them on. Their names 
were Kanthan, Nilan, Muthukaran, Kuruthi, Andan, Nicakan, Chandan, 
Mundan, Mathukayidavan, Tikannan, Puhaikannan and Mali.	 (349)

A sea of  asuras, a thousand in number, came on; there were asuras with 
elephant heads and asuras with impenetrable armour; there were asuras 
with the head of  a horse, and others with the great head of  bull; there 
were the sons of  Kala7 and there were asuras from whose face shot out 
tongues of  flame; there were asuras with the heads of  roaring, leaping 
tigers, and others with the head of  a lion.	 (350)

Of  that number, half  rode prancing steeds, and half  the remainder rode 
elephants; the number of  warlike chariots, drawn by swift horses was 
half  that of  those fierce elephants.8 On they came amidst the din of  
fifes and drums with ghouls and black crows dancing about them [in 



108 Arunachala Puranam

expectation of  a feast], and a tangle of  white banners fluttering above, 
until they drew near to Parvati’s grove.	 (351)

Observing the approach of  the murderous asura, the rishis were 
afraid, and came to Uma in great distress begging for sanctuary. Uma, 
eradicating their fears, turned to the noble Durga9 and addressed her 
with affection, saying, “Take up your blade and slay him!” Whereupon 
Durga took her leave and departed.	 (352)

Dust filled the sky as, with Durga mounted upon a lion, the vanguard 
of  the army marched out, with Arunanayaki to the fore, and Kali and 
Tuntumi to the rear. With these came the hosts of  Parvati, the Bhairavas 
on their dog vahanas, and Brahmani and the rest of  the Seven Mothers, 
riding on their respective vahanas.10	 (353)

Advancing, they slew in an instant half  of  Mahisa’s furious army, using 
stone pestles, trees, whole mountains, boulders, an assortment of  
weapons and even their bare hands. But the leaders of  powerful Mahisa’s 
army and Mahisa himself, like a great mountain, returned to the attack 
and drove Durga’s entire army from the field.	 (354)

The four saktis, Tuntumi, [Kali, Mathanki and Kandi, all] were put to 
flight; the Seven Mothers – She who bears a trident11, She whose throat is 
adorned by the poison halahala12, Varahi, She of  the six faces13, She who 
wields the discus in her hand14, Indirani and She of  the holy Vedas15 – all 
were nowhere to be seen; the Bhairavas in their dog vehicles retreated 
in disarray; in despair Durga cried out, “Uma!”	 (355)

As soon as She cried out, “Uma, our help and support!” sixty-four crores 
of  yoginis rose into the heavens with a thunderous roar and destroyed 
Mahisa’s army with a hail of  fire and thunderbolts, which resembled the 
deluge of  fire at the universe’s end. Anger flared in Mahisa and the leaders 
of  his army, who were standing nearby, and they rushed into battle.	(356)

Arani16 slew Arusikan and Varani17 slew Kanthan as he attacked; 
spear-wielding Karani18 killed Karan and Narani19 took the dear life of  
Narakan.	 (357)



Parvati becomes the left half  of  Lord Siva 109

Trident-wielding [Camunti] slew Mindan, and boar-headed [Varahi] slew 
Candan; the Swallower of  the poison halahala [Maheswari] destroyed 
Mathu, and Lakshmi took the precious life of  Nilan.	 (358)

Fair Tuntumi wielded her club, taking the head of  Sonidavunan 
completely off. Arunachalai, whose hand displays the conch, attacked 
and slew Agnikannan.	 (359)

Both Kayidavan and Pukaikannan met their deaths, slain in battle by 
Sundari. [Durga], she whose spear was festooned with gobbets of  
dripping flesh, espied the powerful head of  Mahisa, and went to confront 
him in battle.	 (360)

Attacked by the powerful buffalo-headed Mahisa, the Seven Mothers 
used their weapons to ward off  his missiles. Then Durga rode to the 
attack, mounted on her lion vahana, and, taking the conch in her hand, 
gave a blast that shook mount Meru itself.	 (361)

Even as the sound of  her conch rang out across the battlefield, all 
the asuras without exception fell into a stupor. The two (remaining 
combatants, Durga and Mahisa) poured down upon each other a rain 
of  arrows, gained through great and hard-won boons.	 (362)

But for every arrow that Durga fired, there was an arrow of  Mahisa’s 
to smash it asunder. Then Durga unleashed two fearsome arrows, like 
the jaws of  the crescent moon, to embed themselves in Mahisa’s head.
	 (363)

Those two arrows unleashed a surging flood of  hot red blood, which 
poured down like molten copper; they were arrows, but their curving 
shape made them look like horns growing out of  his head.	 (364)

Mahisa snapped victorious Durga’s bow in two, and She embedded the 
bow in his forehead, along with the arrows (she had fired). A flame like 
the fire from Lord Siva’s third eye leapt up from the brow of  Mahisa, 
firer of  a multitude of  powerful arrows.	 (365)



110 Arunachala Puranam

Would Durga easily tolerate the destruction of  her deadly bow Durcapam? 
No! In a transport of  rising fury She launched a fiery discus, resembling 
the angry onslaught of  Yama himself, and sent the severed head of  that 
sinner tumbling to the ground.	 (366)

In spite of  being chopped to pieces by Durga’s discus, Mahisa did not 
perish. Through the enduring power of  his previous tapas, he appeared 
as a powerful elephant, dripping with the threefold juices of  the rut, 
and returned to the attack.	 (367)

Durga’s lion mount leapt onto the elephant’s head, scooping out its 
brain and eating it. But Mahisa was still strong and, taking up his club, 
launched it once more. The Queen of  the demon hordes countered it 
with her sharp spear.	 (368)

When that poison-oozing spear landed, death came to the elephant 
headed One, defeated in the furious battle; he crumbled to nothing like 
an atti tree hollowed out by a million beetles.	 (369)

After he had fallen, he returned once more to the attack in the form 
of  a horse[-headed asura], taking up his powerful club and striking 
out. But Durga sliced through it with her sword and took off  his 
head.     	 (370)

Next Mahisa assumed the form of  fire, then of  water; then of  black 
darkness; he assumed the form of  lotus-seated Brahma; he assumed 
the form of  Lord [Vishnu], consort of  the maiden [Lakshmi] who sits 
upon the lotus. All this he did out of  the power of  illusion.	 (371)

Seeing this, what was Durga to do? Realising that he was using his power 
of  illusion to torment her, She became troubled, and held the holy feet 
of  Uma firmly in her heart. No sooner had She done so than a voice 
was heard, speaking from the heavens:	 (372)

“You must strike off  Mahisa’s head, not when he is wearing one 
of  his illusory disguises, but when he is in his natural form. Then, 
when you trample on it with your immaculate feet, his evil power 



Parvati becomes the left half  of  Lord Siva 111

of  delusion will be destroyed.” On hearing these words Durga was 
much heartened. 	 (373)

After battling with Mahisa in countless illusory forms, Durga despatched 
them all with her trident, whose blades resembled long, pointed leaves. 
When the wicked asura, assuming once more the form of  a buffalo, took 
up arms, She cut off  [his head], toppling it to the ground, and trampled 
on it with her feet.	 (374)

Was it such a great achievement that unreal maya should be dispelled by 
those matchless feet, as they were placed [on his head] and trampled 
[on it], when the mere thought of  them is enough to eradicate, not only 
maya, but anavam and much-bruited kamiyam too?	 (375)

The wise gods poured down a rain of  blossoms; buffalo-headed Mahisa 
attained the true state of  liberation, and at his flawless ruddy throat a 
shining lingam was seen to appear.	 (376)

After taking her aim at the throat of  Mahisa, guardian of  the nine 
cloud(-girt) continents, [and slicing through it], Durga saw (that symbol 
of) the Lord at his throat, and grasped it in her hand.	 (377)

Taking it with her, She went to Uma, making obeisance at her lotus-like 
feet, and giving Her (that symbol of ) the Lord. Uma, She whose locks 
are beset by flying beetles, accepted it. Out of  his love for Her, the Lord 
remained firmly stuck to her hand.	 (378) 

When mother [Parvati] saw this, and asked in surprise, “Why does the 
Lord deem it fitting to cling thus to my hand?” [Gautama], he who had 
cursed Indra with a thousand eyes, replied, covering his mouth with his 
hand:	 (379)

“In former times fifty rishis with Agastya at their head went to visit 
Varamuni, but he, convinced of  the greatness of  his own tapas, was 
disrespectful towards them. Those rishis, whose tapas was as precious as 
gold, turned to him angrily and said, ‘For your sin of  thus disrespecting 
us, your next birth will be as a black buffalo, despised by all.’	 (380)



112 Arunachala Puranam

Hearing the bitterness of  the words spoken that day by those fifty 
performers of  the most arduous tapas, that great tapasvin [Varamuni] was 
greatly afraid. Rising up he made obeisance to them, saying, ‘May you 
be gracious and pardon my error!’ to which they replied, ‘During your 
lifetime as the buffalo-headed asura, Mahisa, Uma will be performing 
tapas, desiring a fitting station as the left half  of  the Lord. It is She who 
will in an instant remove this curse.’	 (381)

And so it was that, in the manner they had described, Varamuni was born 
into the great clan of  the asuras, and became a king with the noble name 
of  Mahisasura. One day, during the time he was exercising his cruel rule 
as king, he entered a forest to indulge the sin of  hunting. Rushing hither 
and thither he had become exhausted, afflicted by a burning hunger, 
when he chanced upon a rishi by the name of  Mannatan, engaged in 
the steadfast performance of  worship of  the Lord.	 (382)

Mahisa swallowed down Mannatan along with the lingam he carried 
in his hand and went happily on his way. Subsequently, that ( lingam 
in the form of) Lord Siva remained stuck in his throat, a circumstance 
which conferred great eminence upon Mahisa. For surely any body that 
wears a form that is Lord Siva himself  must itself  be the very form of  
the Lord, must it not? Accordingly a karmic debt was incurred [in the 
killing of  him]. If  you bathe in the nine holy waters, that debt will be 
discharged,” concluded Gautama.	 (383)

As soon as the pious rishi finished speaking, a voice was heard from 
the heavens, saying, “All those nine waters of  rare potency will come to 
this place; make sure by your actions that they do so!” At this Parvati 
turned to Durga, whose face is like a lotus blossom, and said, “May you 
pierce [the earth] with the sword in your hand, and bring forth the river 
Ganges!” [Durga], bearer of  the mighty conch, bowed to Parvati, then 
drove her sword into the ground.	 (384)

When She drew the sword out [of  the ground], the nine holy rivers, 
and other good rivers also, sprang forth, along with many tanks with 
the finest and sweetest of  waters to assuage the torment of  thirst. 
For a whole month Parvati bathed in those waters, so that her sin was 



Parvati becomes the left half  of  Lord Siva 113

dispelled. That emblem of  Lord Siva, which had clung stubbornly to 
Her, fell from her hand. With joy in her heart She returned to the shore 
of  the tank as the gods sang praises.	 (385)

That (emblem of) Siva, which is both with form and without form20, She 
installed on the western side [of  that tank] and performed puja before 
it. Upon Him who removes our good and evil karma, She bestowed the 
title and name of  Cleanser of  my Sins, and upon that great and blessed 
tank She bestowed the holy name of  Sweet Khadga Tirtham.21 Then She 
made her way to the temple of  gold to worship Lord Arunagiri, the 
jewel of  gurus.	 (386)

Bathing in the tank of  Brahma22, who sits upon a fragrant lotus blossom, 
smearing herself  with holy white ash, joining her pink palms above her 
head, and worshipping in the manner prescribed by Gautama, who 
walked close at her side, She entered within, drinking in with her eyes 
the deeply refreshing ambrosia [of  the Lord]. Then, prostrating at full 
length, She gave praise, before rising up again, singing and dancing in 
a transport of  ecstasy, rejoicing with melting heart and weeping and 
sighing deeply. Praising the Lord, She performed pradakshina of  his 
shrine, giving voice to her request.	 (387)

On the holy day in the month of  Karttikai23, as prescribed by the Saiva 
Agamas, praises were offered to Him who bears on his forehead an eye 
of  fire, at which time a blaze sprang up on the summit of  the mountain, 
and within that fire the Lord arose as a bright effulgence, as bright as 
many crores of  suns and impossible to describe; speaking the words, 
“Fair maid, you shall perform pradakshina of  this excellent mountain,” 
it instantly disappeared.	 (388)

The rishi [Gautama], who once turned his own wife [Ahalya] to stone24, 
said, “You should make pradakshina as instructed.” Then, with the Vedas 
thundering out praises like the roar of  the ocean, and with the sound 
of  kettle drums, trumpets, cymbals, mallari, turiya, tuntumi and mattala 
drums filling the wide skies, Parvati, joining her lotus-like hands together 
above her head, began to walk.	 (389)



114 Arunachala Puranam

Damsels of  the heavenly realm with Rambha and Urvasi at their head 
performed dances; mountain nymphs with plump breasts like young 
coconuts strewed swathes of  flowers at her feet; water nymphs with 
voices as sweet as sugarcane waved undulating yak-tail fans; maidens 
from the nether realms with smiles that would make iron melt and flow 
held out jewelled lamps in their extended arms.	 (390)

[Lakshmi], she who resembles a peacock in a hall of  lotus blossoms, 
Pulomaja25, and [Saraswati], she who sits upon a fragrant white lotus 
blossom, removed thorns and pebbles from her path; the Maiden Earth 
reached out her hand [to support her]; celestial maidens and the wives 
of  great rishis made for her a path, sprinkling the perfumed pollen of  
many flowers to soak up the mud created by rivers of  nectar.	 (391)

Ganga joined her hands together and cried, “Your two feet must be 
burning; I beg you to pardon me!” Reaching the fair south east quarter, 
She turned to face the Mountain of  Fire, offering worship and praises. 
Passing the southern quarter and then the [south west] quarter of  Niruti, 
She came to the western quarter. There it was that the Lord whose glory 
is ever unfailing vouchsafed to her the vision of  Himself, mounted upon 
a young bull, then disappeared from sight.	 (392)

Worshipping the dancing feet of  Lord Annamalai, which are praised 
in the four directions, She passed the western quarter, and then [the 
north western quarter of] Lord Vayu. Approaching the northern quarter 
and worshipping with love, She passed the [north eastern quarter of] 
Lord [Isanya], who rides the bull, and then came joyfully to the eastern 
quarter. In thus circumambulating the mountain it was as if, once again, 
She performed the ritual of  walking around the sacred fire, as She had 
done at the time of  her marriage to the Lord.26	 (393)

All the four directions were filled with the sound of  the conches in the 
[thousand] hands of  Bhanukampa, with the blast of  tall Vishnu’s conch, 
with the strains of  Narada’s lute, and with the rattle of  celestial drums. 
The gods themselves poured down a rain of  nectar-drenched blossoms.
	 (394)



Parvati becomes the left half  of  Lord Siva 115

With the tall rays of  the young crescent moon diffusing in His long 
matted locks, with the jewel in the hood of  the termite hill dwelling 
cobra spreading its subdued light, and with a twisted sacred thread 
shining on his breast, as if  it were a measuring line to mark off  the half  
of  his body that was to be given to Her who gave birth to the world, 
the Great One, the unborn and undying Source, whom none of  the 
Vedas can fathom, the deathless Lord, appeared mounted upon the bull, 
blissfully bestowing his grace. [Parvati], the personification of  Dharma 
itself, bowed at his feet and worshipped Him. Then did the Lord who 
has renounced all address Her in the following manner:	 (395 & 396)

“Manifesting as female and male, we have thus divided the beings of  
this world. I became the lingam, and you the pedestal27 just below it. 
But what you think of  as ‘I’ and ‘You’ are not in reality two. Coexisting 
like the tree and its inner core, We dwell as one.	 (397)

You gave up your body entirely for Us and took birth on the Himalaya 
mountain. It is only just that We should confer upon you one half  of  
Our own body. Maiden, may you dwell as the left half  of  Ourself!” Thus 
in grace did He embrace Her with his fair arms, as merging together, 
they became one.	 (398)

On one side were ruddy braided locks, stacked one upon the other, 
and on the other, a lady’s curls; on one side was a garland of  kondrai 
blossoms, and on the other, a fragrant flower garland; on one side was 
a sharp-pronged trident, and on the other, a blooming water lily; on one 
side was a sturdy warrior’s ankle ring, and on the other, a delicate lady’s 
anklet.	 (399)

One side was green in colour, the other was of  a coral hue; on one side 
was a girdled breast, and on the other, a fine bare chest; one hand formed 
the varada mudra 28, and the other the abhaya mudra 28, which removes all 
fear. Observing their loving union, the gods bowed down in worship.
	 (400)

Those who read this noble history or hear it read will, like the rishi 
[Agastya], who dwells on mount [Pothiyam], girt by sandalwood trees, 



116 Arunachala Puranam

come to the further shore of  the Tamil ocean. They will flourish in the 
world with their offspring and all their clan. Free of  the fetters of  this 
worldly bond, they will attain to the deathless state of  liberation.  (	401)

This is the history as my Father related it to me,’ said [Nandi] to the 
great rishi [Markandeya]. Then, when Markandeya arose, bowed down 
in worship, and begged him to tell the tale of  how Vajrangada Pandiyan 
attained the lofty state of  liberation, Nandi was pleased to reply in the 
following manner:	 (402)



References

1.	 The precise references to the exploits of Mahiṣa in this and the two following 
verses are not known. In general terms, he was created invincible by all men 
and gods, and took advantage of the fact to invade heaven and defeat and drive 
out all the gods.

2.	 Chandra – the moon is identified with the Vedic lunar deity Soma (literally 
juice). The Soma name refers particularly to the juice or sap in plants and thus 
makes the moon the lord of plants and vegetation. He is described as young, 
beautiful, fair, two-armed and having in his hands a club and a lotus. He rides 
his chariot (the moon) across the sky every night, pulled by ten white horses or 
an antelope. Compare also v. 189, where the moon is squashed by Vīrabhadra 
in the act of consuming amrita.

3.	 Niruti, Sanskrit Nirṛti is the god who guards the south west direction. See v. 
60, note 48.

4.	 According to the Purāṇas Agastya was born in a pitcher from the semen of the 
Vedic gods Mitra and Varuṇa. When they saw Urvaśī, the celestial nymph, 
they lost control and their semen fell into a pitcher. From it were born two great 
rishis, Vasiṣṭha and Agastya.

5.	 aṇaṅku is a pre-Aryan word for a female deity, often a malevolent demoness in 
the Sangam literature. Here some warlike aspects of Pārvatī seem to be meant.

6.	 See v. 329, note 37. Their Sanskrit names are given as Caṇḍa, Ruru, Kapāli, 
Krodha, Saṁhāra, Unmatta, Bhīṣaṇa and Asitāṅga. 

7.	 These are the Kālakeyas, asuras born to Kāla, by Kaśyapa prajāpati, the son 
of Marīci and Brahmā’s grandson.

8.	 In other words of those 1,000 asuras 500 were mounted on horses, 250 on 
elephants, 125 on chariots, and presumably the rest were on foot.

9.	 Durga is here addressed as Vintai from the Sanskrit vidhyā – knowledge, whilst 
at the end of the verse she is addressed, as often, simply as Kaṇṇi – virgin, 
maiden.

10.	 For the Seven Mothers, see v. 329, note 36. They bear the same emblems and 
ride the same vehicle as their respective male counterparts.

11.	 Cūḻi is Cāmuṇṭī, as carrying a trident.

12.	 Āla kantari is Māheśvari, as having her throat blackened by the poison halahala.



118 Arunachala Puranam

13.	 Āṟu mukattavaḷ is Kaumārī, as having six faces.

14.	 Tikiri kaiyāḷ is Vaiṣṇavī, as wielding the discus.
15.	 Maṟaiyāḷ is Brahmaṇī, as the propounder of the Vedas. 
16.	 Āraṇi = Brahmaṇī. āraṇam is another name for the Vedas.
17.	 Vārāṇi is probably Indrāṇī. vārāṇam means elephant, the mount of Indra. 

18.	 Kāraṇi is probably Kaumārī, since her weapon is the vēl – spear.

19.	 Nāraṇi is Vaiśṇavī.

20.	 The lingam is the manifestation of Lord Śiva that is held to be both with and 
without form, as opposed to his formless, transcendent manifestation, and his 
manifestation with form, as the guru who bestows grace.

21.	 khaṭkam, Sanskrit khaḍga means sword, dagger, referring to the means employed 
by Durga to open up the ground and release the holy waters. The tank is located 
within the Durga ammaṉ temple which is on the pradakṣiṇa path, just north of 
the north east corner of the main temple.

22.	 The Pirammā tīrttam, Sanskrit Brahmā tīrtha is a square tank situated in the 
fourth prakāram – avenue of the Aruṇācaleśvarar temple. It is thought to be 
especially holy as Pārvatī bathed in it before going to worship Aṇṇāmalaiyār.

23.	 This event is celebrated each year on the full moon day in the month of Kārttikai, 
when a beacon is lit upon the summit of the mountain at the culmination of a 
festival which lasts for 17 days in all.	

24.	 See v. 216, note 32. In one version of this story Ahalyā is cursed by Gautama 
to be turned into stone. In that version Indra is cursed to lose his testicles.

25.	 Pulōmacā, Sanskrit Pulomajā, refers to Indrāṇī, the wife of Indra, as the daughter 
of the asura Puloma.

26.	 For information on the Guardians of the Eight Directions, see note 48 on p. 23.

27.	 pīṭam means pedestal; the specific term for the base upon which the lingam rests 
is āvuṭaiyāḷ or āvuṭaiyār, ‘the receptacle representing divine energy, wherein 
the Liṅga is placed.’ Tam. Lex. vol. 1 p. 252.

28.	 For the varada mudra, the right hand, palm facing out, is directed downward. 
It signifies the granting of boons. For the abhaya mudra, see v. 263, note 5.



Chapter 6

வச்சிராங்கத பாண்டியச் 
சருக்கம்

The Story of Vajrangada Pandiyan






‘[The Pandiyan land] is the rich land, watered by the Vaigai river, where 
for many long days the north and south winds, wandering through 
groves of  sandalwood trees with fragrant petals, and imbibing the sweet 
flavour of  the noble Tamil land, arrive together, like maidens who in 
their swaying gait resemble dancing peacocks.	 (403)

It is the land where the luxuriant screwpine bloom profusely, mistaking the 
tiny waists of  maidens with smouldering eyes for bolts of  lightning; it is the 
land of  the rich Vaigai river, on whose banks the jasmine blossoms, mistaking 
the dark locks of  doe-eyed maidens for the onset of  night.	 (404)

The king of that rich land was a king amongst kings, the brave warrior 
who wielded the discus that dislodged Lord Indra’s crown1. He is the one 
who inscribed [his fish ensign] on the foreheads of  the elephants of  
the eight directions, so that it resembled a third eye on the forehead of  
immaculate southern Kailash. 	 (405)

In his physical form and other qualities he rivalled the God of  Love in 
comeliness. He possessed a clear understanding of  the Agamas and other 
holy scriptures. He was a loving devotee of  Him who shares his form 
with the maiden [Parvati]. His shoulders were shoulders that damsels 
with honeyed speech longed to embrace.	 (406)

His glory and renown were greater even than that of  the Himalaya 
Mountain, which is revered both in heaven and upon the earth. He 
ruled the earth, ever mindful of  the fragrance of  the bright atti garland 
hidden in the tangled forest of  the Lord’s matted locks.	 (407)

His head was ever bowed to the holy feet of  our Lord, who wears in his 
locks his own Master [the moon]2. No one was his equal in recognising 
and returning true acts of  friendship.	 (408)

His royal court was strewn with pearls heaped high, given by those who 
had scooped them out of  the conch-strewn ocean, just as the seeds of  
his glory were heaped and strewn across all the far ten directions.     (409)



122 Arunachala Puranam

The sun steering his chariot through the heavens was like a ship sailing on 
the ocean of  the glory of  that victorious king, whilst the deep red sky was 
like the strands of  red coral spreading through that ocean, and the Guardians 
of  the Eight Directions were like sea monsters [swimming in it].	 (410)

Replete with virtue, he ruled the world [supported on the head] of  the 
venomous serpent [Adisheshan], as all creatures, moving and unmoving, 
rejoiced. The polished bands of  warrior anklets adorned his legs. A king 
he was, and his name was Vajrangada.	 (411)

When it is said that he captured even the rainclouds3, which he conjured 
up through his generosity, is it any wonder that he imprisoned many 
kings whom he defeated in battle?	 (412)

At one time that king of  established renown, conceiving a desire for 
the hunt, rode his charger into the northern lands with multitudes of  
soldiers, elephants and horses thronging at his side.	 (413)

When he appeared on his noble, prancing steed, elephants with their 
long trunks stampeded in terror, like the dark hordes of  night fleeing 
in fear in all directions upon the coming of  the King of  the lunar race.4
	 (414)

Possessing the eminence of  the great lunar race, he slew numerous 
proud and beautiful tigers. He who ruled, taking a sixth part in tribute, 
killed the elk he met on his path.5	 (415)

He who had slain the hosts of  king Cheraman, killed entire herds of  
deer. He who lovingly fostered the learned assembly [in Madurai] trapped 
many groups of  stags.5	 (416)

With his chariots, horses, footsoldiers, and elephants, from whose temples 
the juices of  the rut rained down in torrents, all lagging behind, he saw a 
jungle musk deer6 and went in pursuit of  it, alone on his steed.	 (417)

As that musk deer came out of  the jungle and bounded swiftly on, 
circling noble Arunachala with the mountain to its right, the king 



Vajrangada Pandiyan 123

followed in hot pursuit, urging on his steed. Suddenly that scented musk 
deer fell to the ground before his very eyes.	 (418)

As that terrified musk deer keeled over, like someone who had drunk 
poison, and departed its body, it took on a delightful appearance and 
ascended heavenward in a celestial chariot with beautiful celestial 
vidyadharas singing praises.	 (419)

The king fell from his horse, and the horse fell with him. Abandoning 
its equine shape and assuming a form that no artist could ever paint, it 
rose up, its dazzling form mounted on a chariot in the form of  a lotus 
blossom.	 (420)

There at the foot of  the Red Mountain [the king], he who was like an 
elephant with its powerful trunk, fell to the ground near to the legs of  
his horse, grieving as once that king of  Lanka [Ravana], grieved. Yet 
who is there who has never fallen when in pursuit of  a fierce wild beast?
	 (421)

Losing their identities as bounding musk deer and horse, and assuming 
their former identities, Kantisali, and Kaladhara7 were overcome with 
joy, embracing with deep affection and sniffing the crown of  each other’s 
head, with tears streaming from their eyes.	 (422)

Seeing those two brave figures standing there, that king of  kings, whose 
distress was greater even than that which he caused to his own enemies, 
was filled with anguish and sorrow, wondering how all this could have 
come to pass.	 (423)

The two vidyadharas, mounted in their celestial vehicles, approached the 
helpless king and gestured with their hands, saying, “Fear not, fear not!” 
Greatly relieved, the Pandiyan king spoke out:	 (424)

“You who follow the path of  virtue, who are you? What was the initial 
cause of  this sorrowful state of  affairs, and how has it been remedied 
now? Please explain!” he said, to which Kaladhara replied:	 (425)



124 Arunachala Puranam

“King of  Madurai, where grows the finest paddy, we two are matchless 
vidyadharas. His name in Kantisali, and mine, Kaladhara. Close friends, 
we used to sport together.	 (426)

Like Kama and Vasantan, we were quite inseparable, spending all our 
time with each other. During that time, as a consequence of  our deeds in 
former births, we travelled to the slopes of  lofty Mount Meru.     (427)

There we entered a flower garden where all the trees poured down 
copious blossoms; they had grown up lovingly watered by the hot 
tempered rishi Durvasas, who dwelt in his ashram nearby.	 (428)

While we were there he [Kantisali] trampled on some small shrubs, and 
I, for my great sins, plucked some fresh blossoms from them. Seeing 
this Durvasas came after us, sparks of  fire shooting from his eyes, and 
his long matted locks, unbound, flowing behind.	 (429)

With beads of  sweat standing out like fine pearls on his forehead and his 
soft lips quivering like coral, frequently tugging up his upper garment as 
it slipped off  his shoulders, he spoke these words, vivid sparks of  fire 
shooting from his mouth:	 (430)

‘The red rays of  the sun will not come here. No soft southern breeze, 
no swarming bees will ever come near. Here no leaf  ever falls, withers 
or is destroyed. Would anyone lightly enter such a grove?	 (431)

Sinners, by entering the grove which furnishes beautiful flowers for the 
sweet puja of  Him who rides the bull, you have become cotton in the 
fire of  my anger!’ Upon him [Kantisali] he inflicted the poisonous curse 
of  being a horse. To myself, who picked some blossoms, he said, ‘You 
shall be born as a musk deer.8’	 (432)

When the curse of  that wicked one, the fruit of  whose tapas was only 
anger, fell upon us, we were both distraught and approached him crying 
out, ‘Light of  jnana ! Grace itself ! Wealth of  your devotees! Pray tell us 
when this curse, brought upon us by our sins, will be lifted!’	 (433)



Vajrangada Pandiyan 125

His anger subsiding, the curse-dealing rishi spoke, ‘This unique curse 
will be removed by circumambulation of  Sonachala, which is praised by 
men and gods alike; that circumambulation will come to pass through 
the agency of  the king of  the fair Pandiyan land,’ he said.	 (434)

Having spoken thus, he related a story to us. ‘Once the two sons of  Our 
Lord, who stood as a fiery mass of  flame, were begging Him insistently 
for a perfumed [mango] fruit, which formerly the great Lord of  the 
waters [Varuna] had given to Him. The Lord replied, saying, “I shall give 
it to the one who (first) performs a circuit of  the entire earth.”    (435)

Skanda mounted his peacock and set off  to circle the entire sea-girt earth. 
But the one with the dark elephant head arose and performed a circuit 
only of  the Father of  all men, his own father. Lord Siva embraced his 
son and gave him the fruit, and granted him other boons, according to 
his wish. 	 (436)

When spear-wielding Skanda arrived shortly after and asked for the 
fruit, [Lord Siva said], “My son, this elephant-headed one simply walked 
around me on foot. Even if  you circled the seven worlds or performed 
austerities without number, if  you think about it, could that yield a divine 
reward equal to that of  circling me?	 (437)

Those who daily perform pradakshina of  myself, who bear the form of  a 
red mountain, are greater even than Brahma; they are greater even than 
Vishnu; on consideration, they are greater than you; they are greater than 
our Uma; Son, they are greater even than me!” Thus spoke He who is 
greater than all.	 (438)

“Even the deathless gods will not perform puja to this flawless mountain 
which is my very own form; the sweetest puja that can be offered to me 
is that which is lovingly offered to those who have performed pradakshina 
of  that mountain. Even the misdeeds of  which they are ashamed will 
be sins performed by my own self.”	 (439)

Thus did the Lord speak.’ With these words the rishi [Durvasas] 
departed. Subsequently we took birth, as I have explained, as musk deer 



126 Arunachala Puranam

and horse. Today, O Lord of  the southern land, we have completed 
pradakshina because of  you. Regaining our former lightning-bright forms, 
we have mounted our fine celestial vehicles.	 (440)

This is our story. As for you, you have incurred suffering because you 
circled this mountain of  fierce fire riding a horse. If  you perform puja 
this consequence too can be annulled.” Speaking thus he and Kantisali 
departed to their home.	 (441)

Following the king’s tracks his elephants, horses, chariots and foot 
soldiers along with his ministers came and crowded around him. 
Sorrow departed from the Lord of  the land where the Vaigai river flows, 
nurturing with [waters] sweet as the juice of  the sugarcane coconut trees, 
groves of  areca nut trees and banana trees, which reach up to touch the 
white moon of  heaven.	 (442)

Without delay he sent for his son Ratnangada and said, “Guard this 
land like our forefather Bharata!” Almost before he heard those words, 
[Ratnangada], who was sworn to virtuous conduct, took his leave and, 
surrounded on four sides by elephant chariots, returned to the city [of  
Madurai] and exercised his kingly rule.	 (443)

With Ratnangada daily loading up elephants with pearls, gold from the 
revenues of  his just rule, and gold exacted as tribute from his enemies, 
and sending them to his royal father, Vajrangada performed great service 
in the form of  delightful puja to the Lord who wears the crescent moon 
in his matted locks.	 (444)

Having completed this service, his mind bent on tapas, he built a palm 
leaf  hut to one side of  Gautama’s ashram. One day as he was performing 
a circuit of  the mountain in loving devotion to Him who bears the poison 
in his throat, the destroyer of  our sins, He who never reveals himself  
to the deceitful, appeared before that Pandiyan king.	 (445)

When he saw the Lord appear before him mounted upon the bull, with 
the fair crescent moon (in His locks), with eyes that poured forth the grace 
which bestows true knowledge, with four arms, and with the deer and 



Vajrangada Pandiyan 127

axe in his lotus-like hands, he cried out, “When shall I be released from 
the painful prison of  my sins?” and bowed down with deep reverence.
	 (446) 

“You whose name is Annamalai! Rider of  the young bull! You who hold 
the cool waters (of  the Ganges) in your matted locks!  You to whom 
we all belong! You whose forehead bears a third eye! You who dwell 
upon Mount [Kailash], which cannot be known by those who suffer 
delusion and cannot escape their karma! Consort of  Unnamulai Uma!
	 (447)

Bound as I was by evil deeds, in going around you upon a horse I did 
that which should not be done. May you absolve me of  this sin!” Thus 
did he speak. Hearing the words of  one who was free of  falsehood, the 
five-faced One, the Rider on the young bull, pouring forth his grace, 
spoke these words:	 (448)

“King! Lord of  the Sacrifice!9 That day [in a former birth], failing to 
show respect for me, you hurled your thunderbolt at me. On account 
of  that you were born as a man and endured this suffering. Now you 
may return to the glorious realm as the King of  the gods.” Thus, having 
dispensed his boon, the Lord disappeared from sight.	 (449)

Such is the excellence of  Arunachala. Those who fix their thoughts 
upon Arunai’s city will not endure the sorrow of  death. They will come 
to share Lord Siva’s form,’ said Nandi. [Markandeya], he whose tapas 
vanquished Death himself, said, ‘Ocean of  compassion, [through you] 
we have gained salvation,’ and fell at his feet in worship.	 (450)

Those who comprehend so much as a single letter of  the matter of  
this story, and those who read it in the company of  worthy persons will 
attain to union with Sivam. As for those who study it morning, noon 
and evening, and those who enquire into it and grasp its meaning, is it 
any wonder that they will obtain the state of  final liberation?’	 (451)



References

1.	 A puzzling statement since, as we shall see later, Vajrangada is actually an 
incarnation of Indra.

2.	 The Pandiyan kings are praised in literature as descendants of the lunar race, 
of which the ancient king Yadu was fifth in line of descent. Such claims were 
made by a number of royal dynasties of the Indian subcontinent.

3.	 The just rule of the king is synonymous with rain, both of which bring prosperity 
and happiness to the land. See Tiru-k-kuṟaḷ, v. 545:  

	         .

	 In the land of the king who wields his sceptre with justice, there will be both 
rains and rich harvests.

4.	 The moon is meant. See the note to v. 407.

5.	 Tamil text: in verses 415 and 416 the first and second, and third and fourth, 
lines of each verse are identical in the first three feet, but the words are used 
in different meanings. In the first and third lines a quality of the king’s rule is 
described, and in the second and fourth lines, his prowess in hunting is described. 
Below are the second two lines of v. 416:

	                                     

	 Lovingly        of the arts       the academy     he who fostered

	                                      .

	 in multitudes   of stags          herds                trapped.
	 Thus many verses whose content may seem trivial or inconsequential in English 

are seen to exhibit great ingenuity and beauty of expression in the original 
Tamil. It is to be noted that such repetition is only allowed in poetry when the 
meanings are different, especially in the first (rhyming) foot.

6.	 In the Aruṇācala Māhātmya the animal followed by the king is a musk deer, 
gandha sāraṅga, literally, scent deer. The Tamil paraphrase however identifies 
it as puṉuku pūṉai – civet cat. The Tamil word used here, nāvi can have either 
meaning.

8.	 The Māhātmya explains the reason for Durvāsas’ choice of incarnation for the 
pair:



Vajrangada Pandiyan 129

	 tad etad pādasañcārair dūṣayan eṣa pātakī hayo bhavatu bhūloke 
paravāhyatvapīḍitaḥ.

	 Therefore, this sinner, who has defiled this [grove] by treading upon it, let him 
become a horse in the world, suffering as a vehicle for others.

	 aparo ’pi ayam atyugre patatu acalakandare, prasūnagandhalobhāt yo 
gandhasāraṅgatāṁ gatah.

	 As for this other one, let him fall into a very fierce mountain cave, he who shall 
become a musk deer through his greedy desire for the fragrance of flowers.

	 Ar. Mā, Utt. Ch. 22, sl. 35-36.

9.	 Maka pati – Lord of the Sacrifice is a name of Indra, as the performer of a 
hundred horse sacrifices. See also v. 215, note 30. Here it is revealed that Indra 
had been cursed to be incarnated as Vajrāṅgada on account of his showing 
disrespect to Lord Śiva.






Chapter 7

வல்லாள மகாராஜன் சருக்கம்

The Story of Vallala Maharaja






‘I have given a full account of  the greatness of  Vajrangada Pandiyan, 
who displayed such devotion [to the Lord]. Now I shall relate to you 
how the Lord came to king Vallalan1 in the form of  a child, and then 
later granted him his grace in the form of  a firm promise.	 (452)

That city, famed for its bejewelled multi-storied palaces, the glory of  
its dense groves which reach up to the sky, and its courtesans who rival 
Arundhati [in their beauty and chaste demeanour] is fair Arunai.	(453)

King Vallalan was a king of  this city, possessed of  such glory. He was 
truthful in his speech, and endowed with virtuous qualities. He watched 
over his subjects with devotion, holding each as dear as his own life. He 
belonged to the Agni lineage, whose fame is hard to describe. Born to 
worship daily the feet of  the Lord, he persevered in that holy service.
	 (454)

He did not covet the possessions of  others. His conduct towards women 
other than his wives was always correct, and he spoke to them as if  they 
were either his younger or his older sisters. Pure at heart, he received 
from his subjects one sixth of  their earnings according to established law. 
With joy in his heart he performed temple service to the black-throated 
Lord.	 (455)

By the king’s command, cattle and tigers would gather in the same places, 
dwelling together and drinking from the same waters. All would listen 
as Brahmin preceptors recited the Vedas. To obtain the grace of  the 
primal Lord, the citizens would adorn their city so that it was a wonder 
to behold. In the streets maidens would sprinkle water and trace out 
magnificent kolams.	 (456)

Each month rain fell three times and agriculture flourished with unfailing 
abundance. Those who asked for food were invited on the spot and 
generously given food with the six flavours. The people received blessings 
by doing service to great tapasvins and supplying them with all they asked 
for. In the temple of  the just Lord they lit ghee lamps and performed 
puja in perpetuity.	 (457)



134 Arunachala Puranam

It was during this time when that great city flourished in this way through 
the divine will of  the Lord that the king grew despondent, since, in spite 
of  enjoying every felicity, he had no son to speak his name.2       (458)

“Is there any fault in the puja I offer to the Lord? Or is there any failing in 
the justice I administer in this land? Or else, amongst the flowers picked 
for puja, were there some that were defective? Tell me, my ministers, 
what is to be done!” he said.	 (459)

“My Lord if, with love and steadfast faith, we perform acts of  charity 
to all those poor who petition us, then, through the deep compassion 
of  the three-eyed Lord who protects and rules us, a son will be born,” 
[his ministers replied].	 (460)

“In order that my name may be glorified, you shall at once raise a 
beautiful banner up to the heavens, and arrange for fine drums to be 
beaten, proclaiming, ‘To those who enter within this city, where the fair 
Lord of  Arunai dwells in all his splendour, we shall give whatever they 
may ask!’ ” [replied the king].	 (461)

Then the ministers said, “Hear us, king whose glory flourishes 
throughout the world! Is it proper that we should give away to elders, 
to hymn singing bards, and to devotees who worship the holy feet of  
the Lord praised by the sastras, all they have a mind to ask for?”	 (462)

The king replied, “Is it likely that anyone will ask for something which 
does not exist on the earth? And if  they did ask, no one would be able 
to give it. Therefore do as I have commanded with all speed.” At this 
his ministers planted the banner and caused drums to be beaten whose 
din rose up to the realm of  the gods.	 (463)

Hearing about the banner which the king had raised in Arunai’s city 
where Lord Siva dwells, people came from all over the world – old 
people, poor people carrying the kavadi3, along with great tapasvins of  
high renown, Brahmins, jogis4, virasaivas, minstrels and beggars – all 
crowding together in due order to receive gifts that supplied their needs.
	 (464)



Vallala Maharaja 135

To those who came asking for money to pay for a marriage a thousand 
gold coins were given. Then, to those who said, “O king, please give 
us money to redeem the houses, land and jewels we have pledged,” 
he at once gave two thousand gold coins, filling them with joy. Then, 
to Brahmin elders who said they wished to perform the sacred thread 
ceremony, upanayana, he gave three hundred gold coins.	 (465)

When others said, “The temple’s surrounding wall and the jewelled 
mantapams within it are in a poor state of  repair. May you grant us, O 
king, the funds to restore them to their proper condition,” he showed 
great devotion in giving 20,000 gold coins, saying, “Go and repair them!” 
In addition, 50,000 gold coins were given to support maths throughout 
the land.5	 (466)

Hearing that the king was indeed, in accordance with his proclamation, 
each day magnanimously bestowing gifts upon the blind and lame, upon 
virasaivas and jogis, upon those who had suffered calamities, upon the 
poor, upon those who endured the endless suffering of  the disease of  
poverty and many others of  a similar kind, the sage Narada made his 
way to that place.	 (467)

When the king heard that this rishi, this great tapasvin, had come 
there, he was filled with great affection. Swiftly descending from his 
throne, that Lord of  the Earth went to meet him, surrounded by his 
ministers.  Praising the holy one, he escorted him back, offered him 
a throne which no flaw marred, and once he was installed upon it, 
began to speak:     	 (468)

“Most excellent of  sages, you who were born from the tapas of  Brahma 
himself, you who sing nobly grasping the vina in your fair hand! Deign to 
apprise me without delay of  the purpose of  your coming here, resting 
your compassionate gaze upon this lowly cur.” To this the ascetic replied:
	 (469)

“O King descended from the ancient line of  Agni, one of  the three 
praised by tapasvins who have attained greatness upon the earth and by 
silent ascetics who have conquered the five powerful senses! Hearing of  



136 Arunachala Puranam

your flawless acts of  charity, I have come here to learn about it. I beg 
you to explain.” To these words of  Narada the king replied as follows:
	 (470)

“Sage great in tapas, who are praised by tapasvins learned in the mantras 
of  the Vedas and sastras, pray listen!  Poor wretch that I am, I have no 
son to speak my name, or to rule my kingdom after me. Accordingly 
I have raised a banner, by token of  which I will with loving kindness 
give to devotees of  Him who shares his form with Her whose locks 
are adorned with dewy blossoms those things upon this earth that they 
desire and request. As for the Lord, I know not what he may do.”    	

(471)

Narada replied, “That those who perform deeply sacred acts of  charity 
on this earth will be granted children is affirmed not only in the dharma 
scriptures, but also by those great ones who are qualified [in the Vedic 
lore]. Therefore through the grace of  the Supreme Guru who protects 
all living beings, wearing with delight the flooding river Ganges and the 
crescent moon in his matted locks, a child will be born to you. Now, O 
king, grant me leave to depart.”	 (472)

Desiring to bring tidings of  the king’s righteous generosity to the 
supreme Lord who reigns upon Mount Kailash, the great sage journeyed 
with love in his heart. On his arrival Narada paid homage to the [second] 
Sankara (Nandi), whose glorious form stood before him, as Lord Siva’s 
celestial hosts stood by singing praises, and then, espying the holy scene 
of  the Lord who wears the crescent moon in his locks, with [gods and] 
rishis crowding all about, he made obeisance and began to speak:       (473) 

“First One, God of  gods, dwelling upon effulgent Mount Kailash, 
praise be to your holy feet! There is one, King Vallalan of  the fair city 
of  Arunai, whose glory shines without flaw. Desiring a son to glorify 
his name, he has raised a banner and proclaimed to the sound of  drums 
that he will freely give to those who ask whatever they desire. Hear then 
of  his greatness!	 (474)

He fosters justice and upholds the truth. Never does he swerve from the 
path of  righteousness. [Like the moon rising up without its blemish], this 



Vallala Maharaja 137

king has arisen over the earth in the form of  the dharma that is free from all 
flaws. He watches over all the creatures of  the earth, esteeming their lives 
no less precious than his own.  He is your own devotee who spends each 
day of  his life crying, ‘O Lord, your holy lotus feet are my refuge!’”	 (475)

Having heard the powerful account uttered by that great sage, and thinking 
to himself, “We shall ascertain for ourselves what the true nature of this 
Vallalan may be,” the peerless Lord ordered the sages who held Him 
in their thoughts, the rishis, and the rest of  the gods to return to their 
respective dwellings.	 (476)

The moment the Lord of  Kailash summoned the king of  Alaka, [Kubera] 
came at once, bearing a hoard of  gold so vast that the inhabitants of  
all the Eight Directions were filled with awe. After he had paid homage 
at the grace-bestowing feet of  the Lord who is adorned with serpents 
and rudraksha beads, [Lord Siva], He whose form is like the ruddy sky, 
deigned to utter a few words:	 (477)

“Hear me, King and Lord of  Alaka!  I am minded to test the strength 
of  the devotion in the heart of  the king who dwells in Arunai’s fair city. 
Therefore you shall joyfully accompany Us, in the guise of  a mature 
disciple bearing great riches,” said the Lord of  Mount Kailash.	 (478)

With Brahma and Vishnu looking on, experiencing a joy which they 
had never known, with the gods pouring down a rain of  blossoms and 
the Vedas singing praises, the Lord, who shines with the Maiden whom 
words cannot describe as half  of  himself, appeared in the form of  a 
Saivite monk, accessible to the praises of  all.	 (479)

The beautiful celestial hosts of  Lord Siva who dwell with Him on 
Mount Kailash all assumed the form of  religious mendicants through 
the goodly grace of  our Lord. Reaching the city of  Vallalan, which is 
glorious upon the earth, they entered in a large group, feted by those 
who knew the ways of  the king.	 (480)

“Are there no mothers of  impeccable chastity, who regard their husbands 
as gods? Are there no handsome and worthy sons?  Are there none who 



138 Arunachala Puranam

will give food to stave off  hunger? Are there no learned monarchs? Are 
there no garlanded maidens who will call us with heartfelt sincerity and 
chastely offer us food?	 (481)

Even if  we were offered gold we would not want it, nor would we want 
fine ornaments if  we were offered them. If  we were given rare and 
precious jewels we would have no desire for them, nor would we place 
any value upon long pearl necklaces. Even if  we were invested with the 
righteous rule of  kings, our hearts would not be in it. But if  someone 
were to give us food and look after us, we would happily eat,” they said.
	 (482)

In the streets of  fine and noble Arunai’s great city, the Lord, who had 
come as a Saivite mendicant, bereft of  the deer he is wont to hold in 
his fair hand, opened the red water lily of  his mouth and raised a cry. 
Calling out, like those who suffer the sharp pangs of  hunger and come 
to receive alms, He made his way to the street of  devadasis 6 whose lips 
are red as kovvai7 fruits.	 (483)

“Maidens, tell me, how much gold would it require for someone to spend 
the night from dusk to dawn, defeating the onslaught of  the flowery 
arrows (of  Kama) in the company of  maidens such as yourselves, with 
carp-like eyes and parrot-like speech; whose countenances resemble the 
moon at the full; whose tresses are covered in posies of  fresh flowers, 
and whose breasts are like the quivering young fruits of  the coconut, 
smeared with fragrant unguents?” He enquired. 	 (484)

“O Lord beyond compare, you who resemble the Supreme One, who 
adorns himself  with hooded serpents! We will not speak falsely, nor 
entertain any thought of  deception. For one whole night of  lovemaking, 
a thousand gold pieces for each girl is the price. If  such be given, we 
will make love, body on body, and repel the attack of  Kama,” [said the 
courtesans].	 (485)

Handing out the gold as the courtesans had stipulated, he assigned each 
girl to the care of  one of  the Saivite monks, signalling to them with his 
eyes, and impressing upon them very strongly with his words, that they 



Vallala Maharaja 139

should not become separated [for the entire night].  Thus did he cause 
the streets of  the courtesans to be illumined with the radiance of  his 
Saivite hosts, a radiance which extended to a distance of  six leagues 
from Arunai’s city, generous granter of  boons.	 (486)

Having made sure that no courtesan remained available, and leaving 
them all engaged in the arts of  lustful Kama, that Saivite monk, (Lord 
Siva) departed with his disciple [Kubera], who had investigated and 
understood the illustrious path of  virtue. As they approached [the palace 
of  the king], the king of  that land, an expert in the art of  archery, saw 
them coming and went to meet them. Making obeisance, he invited 
them inside, seated them honourably and began to speak:	 (487)

“Is it due to the tapas I have performed? Or on account of  whatever 
good deeds I am currently performing upon the earth? When I consider 
the matter, is it to reward me for such generosity [that you come here], 
causing pain to your divine feet of  gold, to subject me to your virtuous 
rule? I shall be honoured to do whatever you request, and to receive 
your glance of  grace.”	 (488)

“Listen, O King, may your kingdom and your just rule ever flourish! I 
shall reveal why I was minded to come to you. If  you furnish me with a 
courtesan to remove the torment caused by the five arrows employed by 
Kama, your fame will shine out over the seven oceans [of  the world],” 
said the Lord.	 (489)

[The King replied,] “Rather than that, I will have a fine marriage arranged 
for you.” Upon hearing this, the venerable Saivite said, “Listen to me 
O King, that would cause me much trouble; but the skill of  courtesans 
in curing the baneful disease of  desire is like buried treasure.”	 (490)

“Learned Saivite, you who are able to impart true knowledge even to 
those who are established in meditation, I shall do as you desire,” said 
the king, and despatched his guards, saying, “Go at once and bring here 
one of  those beautiful devadasis.” Whereupon they set off, faster than 
the wind.	 (491)



140 Arunachala Puranam

Going to the streets where the courtesans lived and entering every house, 
they heard the intimate sounds of  music and dancing, and saw one of  
the Lord’s company, clad in a garland of  kondrai blossoms, besporting 
himself  [with one of  the courtesans]. “Today it won’t be possible,” 
they thought to themselves, and reported back to the king accordingly.
	 (492)

On hearing this the king flew into a rage and turned to his ministers and 
said, “Is it the will of  the Lord that my words should prove false? Or 
is there some defect in the puja we perform? Is it acceptable to say to 
the face of  this Saivite, whose words are pure and true, that a courtesan 
such as he requests is not to be found?”  In response the ministers said:
	 (493)

“Rich and prosperous Lord, please do not be distressed. Humble servants 
as we are, we will go ourselves and bring back a courtesan of  wondrous 
beauty. Let there be an end to your sadness.” With that they made their 
way to the network of  streets where the courtesans lived. Observing 
the fine entertainment that was taking place there, they addressed those 
courtesans, whose perfumed breasts were round like spheres:	 (494)

“In this street where fine damsels with kohl painted eyes dwell in such 
great numbers, is there one who will satiate the lustful desire of  a great 
personage who has requested just this of  our righteous monarch? 
If  there be such, she will spend the rest of  her life wearing jewelled 
bracelets and necklaces and fine ringing anklets, and eating food of  the 
six flavours, along with ghee, curds and milk.”	 (495)

Having heard what the ministers said, those ladies with waists as slender as 
a lightning flash respectfully replied, “We have all already accepted payment 
from these devotees of Lord Siva, to remain with them for the entire night. 
Once this night has passed, we will gladly comply with your request.”  When 
the ministers, greatly distressed in their mind, reported this to the king, he 
spoke once more:	 (496)

“How could something so simple become an impossible task for us?” he 
said, sighing heavily and feeling helpless as many thoughts ran through 



Vallala Maharaja 141

his anxious mind. “Is this the action of  Lord Siva’s grace? (However 
it may be) I will make good my promise to this Saivite monk whose 
appearance recalls Lord Siva himself.” So saying, that king, valiant wielder 
of  the bow in battle, betook Himself  with all speed to [the street of] 
the devadasis.	 (497)

“At this time of  charitable works, when a flag has been raised to the 
heavens to proclaim that we shall give whatever is asked to those who 
request it, I shall give this (entire) city to the courtesan who has the 
kindness to save me, preventing me from breaking the promise I have 
given this day to that fair Saivite monk,” he said.	 (498)

“If  she satisfies the desires of  that venerable one, I shall give her 
elephants, horses and all the gold she desires. I shall give her a palanquin 
inlaid with pearls. Here and now I shall invest her with my rare and 
righteous sceptre to rule this ancient land, and become myself  her 
servant. She will be as my own mother.	 (499)

Sages learned in the Vedas declare that of  all the pleasures to be enjoyed 
in this world, this is the highest. Therefore if  you satisfy the desire of  
the noble Saivite monk who has come to our land, I will even give up 
my own illustrious existence, should you request it. Come then, you 
ladies!” he said.	 (500)

When the courtesans replied, saying, “Faithfully abiding by your 
command, we have accepted money to enjoy the pleasure of  love with 
these pure devotees. Sire, what else can we do?” that Lord of  the earth 
was ashamed and returned to his noble palace.	 (501)

Upon his return to the palace two of  his wives both noticed the changed 
expression upon the king’s face, which resembled the moon [at the 
full]. They were Mallamadevi, and one of  his younger wives, the most 
excellent Sallamadevi. “Our Lord, you who possess the might to protect 
this ancient world, what is the reason for your sorrow? Pray tell us!” 
they asked, prostrating at his feet. [Vallalan], he who walked the path 
of  virtue, replied:	 (502)



142 Arunachala Puranam

“Today a venerable one, desiring the pleasurable company of  a doe-eyed 
maiden, came to me and requested just that from our victorious Self. 
Having agreed to his request, I was distressed because there was not 
a single devadasi available in all of  the city.” Having listened to his true 
account the beautiful [younger] wife said:	 (503)

“King, you who have made a promise to a devotee of  Lord Siva tormented 
by desire! We do not know what you are thinking of  doing. But if  you are 
thinking that I, the younger of  the two, should offer myself  to him, then 
I shall agree to it,” she said. The noble king was overjoyed. He looked 
at her and said, “Fair lady, then shall you retire with that devotee into a 
bedchamber and relieve the suffering caused by the Formless One.” The 
good king informed that venerable one accordingly.	 (504)

Without delay Sallamadevi bathed in perfumed water, dressed in all her 
finery and went into the room. There she seated herself  by his side, and 
sang songs of  exquisite beauty, accompanying herself  on the finest of  
lutes. But when, [after some time], she looked closely at the supreme 
Lord, she saw that his rudraksha clad figure was deeply immersed in 
yogic trance.	 (505)

Next, the young woman, thinking to please that great one, took some 
of  the perfumed water she had used on herself  and sprinkled it over his 
imposing form, and stood attentively nearby. When he did not look at 
her, not even opening his eyes, she became dejected and began to speak:
	 (506)

“Alas, my Lord, is it proper that the promise uttered by a king should 
prove false?” she said, bowing to him. Then, as she gently lay down by 
his side and embraced him, that Supreme One assumed the form of  a 
babe in arms and began to cry, filling her with joy.	 (507)

When the Lord assumed the form of  a baby and began to wail and cry, 
the king came quickly, realising that this was the sport of  the Supreme 
Lord. Taking it in his arms and embracing it, he joyfully kissed it on 
the crown of  the head. But even as he stood there, full of  joy, the Lord 
disappeared.	 (508)



Vallala Maharaja 143

“Lord, can we know the workings of  your divine will? Ignorant fools, 
we cannot! Embodiment of  the real! Three-eyed One! You who are 
the Vedas and the Vedas’ Lord! Pure One! Was it to test us that You 
appeared as a boy child and then disappeared again? What is our destiny 
now, O virtuous Lord?”  Thus did the king and his queen lament. (509)

Even as the king lamented in this fashion, sobbing loudly, the Lord, who 
is praised by those whose austerities are true, appeared, mounted upon 
the bull with his consort Parvati, with the hosts of  heaven crowding all 
about. With Brahma and Vishnu in his retinue, He afforded the vision 
of  Himself  to the victorious king who dwelt in Arunai’s fair city, and he 
in turn lovingly made obeisance to the Lord and praised Him.     (510)

“First One, I seek the refuge of  your holy feet! Light of  the real, with 
the power to protect your devotees upon the earth, I seek the refuge of  
your holy feet! Supreme One whose immaculate, ruddy locks, where the 
Ganges flows in spate, are crowned with the crescent moon! Flawless 
One, I beg you in your grace to grant a son to preserve the just rule of  
this poor wretch!”	 (511)

“Fair king, listen! I myself  became your son and heir.8 Therefore at the 
time of  your death I will perform the Vedic rites for you.” So saying 
Chandrasekhara gave his blessing to the king and returned to Mount 
Kailash. Thereafter the king prospered, virtuously ruling the land 
according to established rule.	 (512) 



References
1.	 The information contained in this, and the following notes in this chapter, is 

taken mainly from the annotated translation published by David Godman on 
his website, a part of which also appeared in the Mountain Path magazine. See 
the Bibliography for details.

	 King Vira Vallalan III was an illustrious king who ruled over the Hoysala 
empire from 1292 till 1342. At its peak it covered a large part of South India. 
Its traditional stronghold was the southern part of the Deccan plateau where 
the capital city Dwarasamudra was located. Around 1315-1316 he made 
Tiruvannamalai his southern capital, and later, around 1327, after the loss of 
Dwarasamudra to the muslim Delhi sultanate, he conducted the remaining 15 
years of his reign from there. His inclusion in the Aruṅāchala Purāṇam would 
seem to be due to the great works of charity he performed in extending and 
renovating the Aruṇāchalēsvarar temple, and supporting its activities, facts 
which are attested in nine epigraphs (carved stone inscriptions). Numbered 
references to these in the notes refer to the definitive edition published by the 
Institut Français de Pondicherry: Tiruvannamalai, a Saiva Sacred Complex in 
South India, volume one, part one, ‘Inscriptions’. Ed. P.R. Srinivasan, published 
in 1990. 

2.	 ‘To speak his name’ means to perform the funeral ceremonies after his death. 
It was traditionally believed that the son had to perform the last rites in order 
to send his father’s soul to the next world.

3.	 The kāvaṭi is either a pole carried on the shoulders for taking offerings to the 
temple, or, as seems more likely in this case, a mendicant’s pole, which he takes 
from house to house to gather offerings.

4.	 jōki is derived from the Sanskrit word yōgin. The Tamil Lexicon describes them 
as ‘a caste of itinerant Telugu mendicants who are dexterous jugglers and snake 
charmers, and claim a profound knowledge of charms and medicine.’

5.	 There is no inscription that authenticates this series of events but there are 
several that show that he was a major patron of all the services that the temple 
provided. Inscriptions 301, dated 1317, and 302 give elaborate details of the 
activities he was supporting: offerings and adornment for the resident deities; 
annual processions of the deities; food for temple servants; support for reciters 
of the Vedas, suppliers of water, servers of areca nut, those who made paste for 
the body, incense burners, washermen, barbers and umbrella makers; works on a 
mantapam; feeding rice to brahmins; providing milk; endowments for festivals, 
and so on.

 	 One surprising omission from the epigraphs is any detailed reference to the 
major building works that King Vallalan is believed to have undertaken in the 



Vallala Maharaja 145

temple. The gopuram on the east side of the fourth prakara is named after him, 
and it is generally accepted that he was responsible for its construction. This is 
a curious omission for a man who liked to boast about his achievements, right 
down to such minor expenditures as six pon (gold coins) to some officials who 
performed services in one of the mantapams. 

     	 Only relatively minor sums are allotted to building works in the nine epigraphs 
that date from his reign. Inscription 301, for example, has a grant of 400 pon for 
‘a variety of construction work,’ while later the same inscription notes a gift of 
800 pon for ‘the worship in the temple and for construction works including the 
wall.’ This may be the wall, mentioned in verse 466, that needed to be repaired. 
It is certainly too small a sum to build the major wall that abuts the Vallalan 
Gopuram.

	 The only clear reference to work on a gopuram comes at the end of epigraph 302, 
dated 1341. There it is stated: ‘for the temple works including the building of the 
gopuram and the enclosure wall built by Tiruppal.’ This is a damaged inscription in 
which the text before and after this statement is missing. There is no mention 
of the sum involved – if it was a large amount it would indicate that a major 
work was in progress – nor is it mentioned which gopuram or which wall. As 
support for the contention that King Vallalan built the gopuram that now bears 
his name and the wall on either side of it, the epigraphic evidence cannot be 
regarded as conclusive. 

	 Both the Arunachala Puranam account and the temple epigraphs from 
King Vallalan’s reign regularly and repeatedly boast of his greatness and his 
accomplishments. The fact that neither of them specifically mentions his role in 
constructing what would then have been one of the biggest and most visible parts 
of the temple complex is strong circumstantial evidence that his contemporaries 
did not associate him with its construction. Be that as it may, popular belief and 
custom now firmly identify the gopuram with King Vallalan. There is even a 
local tradition about the trials the king underwent after its construction. 

	 King Vallalan, after building his gopuram, felt great pride in his achievement. Lord 
Arunachaleswara, noting that the feeling ‘I have built this great gopuram’, was 
strongly rooted inside him, decided to teach him a lesson. There is a ten day festival in 
which Arunachaleswara is paraded each day through the streets of Tiruvannamalai. In 
the first festival after the gopuram had been built, Arunachaleswara initially refused 
to leave the temple via the passage in the centre of the new gopuram. For the first 
nine days of the festival, He always left the temple via a different route. On the 
tenth and last day the king realised his mistake and became more humble. He broke 
down and cried before the Lord, begging Him to use the gopuram for just one day. 
Lord Arunachaleswara saw that the king’s pride had abated and granted his request. 
This particular festival is still celebrated in Tiruvannamalai. To commemorate King 
Vallalan’s attack of pride and his subsequent humility, Arunachaleswarar is only 
taken through the king’s gopuram on the tenth and final day. 



146 Arunachala Puranam

     	 Apart from the Vallala Gopuram and the wall adjoining it, there are other items 
in the temple that are clearly a result of his patronage. There are several statues 
of him, one of which is still garlanded every day. One shows him with a full 
beard, another shows him with one of this queens, and a third as an old man. 
He was also responsible for the Nandi, and its covering mantapam, which lies to 
the east of the Kili Gopuram. On the right-hand pillar there is a carving of King 
Vallalan and on the left-hand pillar there is a carving of the ganda-berunda, the 
imperial emblem of the Hoysalas.

6.	  Young girls became devadasis – servants of god by being consecrated to the 
service of a temple deity. Although they were taught singing and dancing and 
were obliged to perform before devotees, they were often little more than 
prostitutes whose earnings went toward the support of the temple or its priests. 
The British, during their occupation of India, tried to put an end to the practice, 
but were not totally successful. The tradition still lingers on in some areas such 
as northern Karnataka.

7.	 kovvai is a climbing plant, Coccinia indica, noted for its red edible fruit, to 
which the lips are compared in poetry.

8.	 Śiva’s promise to perform the Vedic ritual at the time of the king’s death is 
still remembered and commemorated every year in Tiruvannamalai. In the 
month of Māsi the temple priests read out the news of King Vallalan’s death 
to Arunachaleswara. His image is then carried in a procession to the village 
of Pallikonda Pattu, about three kilometres from Tiruvannamalai, for the 
performance of the king’s annual sraddha rites. The connection between this 
village and the life and death of King Vallalan is no longer known. It is unlikely 
that he lived there since his palace is thought to have been located about a mile 
to the east of the main temple.



Chapter 8

தீர்த்தச் சருக்கம்

Holy Tanks






‘Praising the two holy feet of  the elephant-headed [elder son, Vinayakar], 
brave in battle, and praising the holy feet of  [Murugan], the Lord of  
the mountain tracts1, who destroyed [the demon Surapadman in] the 
ocean whose waves cast up pearls, and slew [the asura Krauncha in the 
form of] a mountain,2 let us hold in our thoughts the emerald-hued 
mountain’s daughter [Parvati] and [her consort] Lord [Siva], who stood 
as a mountain of  fire to banish the tears and grief  of  his devotees, and 
abolishes our (future) births.	 (513)

In this outpouring of  love I have given a summary of  the noble Rudra 
Samhita of  the Siva Purana. Now I shall give an account, as I have 
understood it, of  that which is recounted at great length in the fair Linga 
Purana.3	 (514)

Brahma, who was in the beginning, told it to Sanaka. Sanaka passed it 
on to Vyasa, who recounted it to rishi Suta. Suta in turn told it to the 
flawless rishis [of  the Namisa forest].	 (515)

“I have previously recounted how the great mountain appeared in the 
form of  fire, and how the daughter of  the great [Himalaya] mountain 
merged [with the Lord] becoming [the left] half  of  Him. Therefore, 
leaving that aside, I will now tell the story of  what transpired thereafter.
	 (516)

I shall now speak of  [the greatness of] the tanks [of  Arunachala], and 
of  the virtuous acts performed there; [the greatness of] pradakshina of  
the mountain that manifested there; [the greatness of] its lingams; the 
ignominy of  those who do not worship it; the sin incurred by those who, 
though aware [of  this ignominy] misappropriate that which belongs to 
the Lord; the sins unwittingly committed by those under the influence 
of  the bondage [of  their previous karma]; the sins which are knowingly 
performed there; the manner in which our forefathers became absolved 
[of  such sins], and the delight experienced by a civet cat [through the 
grace of] our Lord.4	 (517 & 518)

[Rishi] Gautama, who was free of  desire [for worldly things] performed 
the Vedic sacrifice in the Lord’s presence. As a result, through [the 



150 Arunachala Puranam

grace of] the Lord with the radiant, holy matted locks, a Brahmin priest 
was born, appearing like the very manifestation of  beautiful Agni. 
[Gautama] paid his respects, and bade him perform puja to the Lord 
of  the Mountain.	 (519)

Thereafter Gautama took seven stalks of  darbha grass and cast them into the 
flames [of  the sacrificial pit]. At once seven maidens appeared with beautiful 
locks, each resembling the goddess Lakshmi. [Seeing these] devadasis standing 
there, he bade them go and perform dance before the Lord. These maidens 
caused many tanks to be built, beautifully adorning the city.	 (520)

Close on the east side of  the Fire Mountain, praised in mantras [by the 
gods] is the Indra tirtham. This was first established by Indra. Through 
the gods of  heaven themselves bathing in it daily, its waters are redolent 
of  the scent of  sandalwood paste, and the fragrance of  garlands made 
from [the blossoms of] the five [flowering][ trees [of  heaven].	 (521)

If  one bathes in this most excellent of  tanks and drinks from it on Tai pusam5, 
the sin of  Brahminicide, repeated ten crores of  times, will be expunged. 
Could it be possible, then, to express its greatness in words? [Indra], the 
king of  the gods, bathed in it and destroyed his evil karma. He obtained the 
boon of  living as long as the sun, ruling over the realm of  the gods.   (522)

The tank called Agni tirtham sits proudly to the south east of  Arunai’s 
great mountain. If  one bathes in it on the full moon in the month of 
Panguni 6, the enduring, sinful karma that comes from (frequenting) 
bejewelled maidens with carp-like eyes and [coral-]red mouths will be 
banished, and (the practice) of  dharma and tapas will be gained. 	 (523)

At one time, when Agni conceived a desire for the highly desirable wives 
of  the seven sages, learned in the Vedas, [his wife] Svaha Devi took the 
form of  those wives and coupled with him. To absolve himself  of  the 
sin he had contemplated, Agni bathed in the very tank which he himself  
had created. 	 (524)

In the south is the Yama tirtham. The body of  the one who bathes in it 
will become of  the form of  shining gold. The diseases of  the body will 



Holy Tanks 151

be reduced to ashes and disappear. In former times Yama, by bathing 
in it, removed the curse of  being made very small, after being pursued 
by the weapon of  Brahma.	   (525)

There is a tank called Niruti tirtham, which must be mentioned. It is in 
the (south westerly) direction of  Niruti7. For those who bathe in it, all 
enmity towards them, however implacable, will end. In former times 
Niruti himself  bathed with devotion in that noble tank and thereby 
[gained the strength] to win victory over a demon with gaping jaws and 
eyes like billowing flame.	  (526)

On the western side of  the flawless Mount is the excellent Varuna 
tirtham. To him who bathes in it with devotion all nine planets will be 
favourable. For it was there that all bathed to obtain their respective glory. 
Proceeding from there in the (north westerly) direction of  Vayu, is the 
Vayu tirtham. The sorrows of  those who bathe in it will all be ended.
	 (527)

In the northerly direction is the Kubera tirtham. The poverty of  those 
who bathe in this will be dispelled. To the east of  this is the Isana tirtham, 
built by [the Asvini devas], the physicians of  heaven. Those who go there 
and bathe in it, be they lower than the meanest outcasts, will in fitting 
manner surpass in eminence all other four castes, and be united with 
Lord Siva.	  (528)

To the south of  the Yama tirtham is the Agastya tirtham. Those who 
look upon the moon-like countenances of  those who have bathed there 
in the excellent month of  Purattaci and drunk its waters, will become 
masters of  Tamil poesy. Beautiful Lakshmi of  peacock-like beauty, and 
the Maiden of  learning, [Saraswati] will be their companions.  	 (529)

Near to the Kubera tirtham is a tank called Vasistha tirtham. By bathing 
there in the month of  Aippaci, that sage gained the foremost place 
among all the rishis. To those who bathe in it the subject matter of  the 
Vedangas8 will become as clear as a nelli fruit in the palm of  the hand, 
and they will surely cross the ocean of  sinful existence.	 (530)



152 Arunachala Puranam

Ganga, spear-wielding Murugan, the elephant-headed [Ganesha], the 
Sun, the mountain’s daughter [Parvati], the one and only Bhairava, the 
[seven] Maidens, the Moon, the eight Vasus, the Lady who dwells upon 
the white lotus, [Saraswati], and the rest of  the gods, all bathe in the 
numerous tanks which surround [the mountain]. Even the Vedas could 
not express the greatness of  a single one of  these.	 (531)
